# Hermes TypeScript migration: provider ports and durable productivity turns

Hermes will finish the six-phase migration in full strict TypeScript. Application code, integrations, persistence, workers, tests and executable scripts are in scope. Generated JavaScript, dependencies, SQL, shell and static assets are outside that source-language requirement. JS/TS coexistence is temporary.

Phases 1–3 provide strict workspace contracts, atomic tools and an isolated durable reminder flow. Phase 4 now supplies the shared multihop loop, operational context summaries and deterministic mixed-domain presentation with checkpoint/recovery integration. Real-provider token, semantic, latency and cost gates remain open. Phase 5 now supplies isolated native and strict structured provider ports using current configured OpenRouter routes, plus local initial-foreground validation. Typed approval/continuation wrappers, background/multimodal parity and other adapters remain open. The production router now provides disabled-by-default V2 admission with selective and global targeting for leased text turns; existing baseline conversations require explicit handoff, and the webhook supplies V2 write authorization through active-lease and authenticated-owner checks. The background reminder scheduler is unchanged.

## Run the foundation checks

```sh
pnpm run typecheck
pnpm run test:agent-core
pnpm run test:unit
pnpm run migration:inventory
```

`typecheck` builds the referenced packages and checks maintained application/script/test TypeScript sources. The packages emit Node ESM and declarations into ignored `dist` folders. Relative imports use `.js` extensions so compiled output runs in Node. The existing application still starts from JavaScript during this phase. TypeScript options follow [strict checking](https://www.typescriptlang.org/tsconfig/strict.html) and [Node module resolution](https://www.typescriptlang.org/tsconfig/module.html).

## Package boundaries

| Package | Implemented foundation |
| --- | --- |
| `@templelabs/contracts` | Validated provider-neutral messages, calls, results and response usage; trusted turn metadata types; atomic reminder/task/calendar inputs and normalized results. |
| `@templelabs/ai` | Provider interface and scripted, network-free mock with transcript validation, cancellation, deadline/output checks and detached request snapshots. |
| `@templelabs/tools` | Validating tool builder, capability-scoped registry, execution states/receipts and twelve initial domain tools. |
| `@templelabs/agent-core` | Opt-in authenticated user selection, pinned active-run engine, limits contract, shared bounded loop with reminder and isolated productivity compositions, and operational context summaries. |

The internal `tool` role is a normalized contract; each real adapter must translate it to its provider protocol. Histories reject orphan results, reused call IDs, missing results and unrelated messages inserted between calls/results. All results from a batch must be present before continuation.

Synthetic fixtures describe reminder creation, reference update and acknowledgement. They verify scripted message/result pairing. They do not prove temporal interpretation, memory resolution or actual database writes; existing domain tests remain the baseline and Phase 2/3 add adapter and end-to-end coverage.

## Phase 2 reminder increment

`reminder_create`, `reminder_read`, `reminder_update` and `reminder_acknowledge` expose separate strict schemas. Identity, locale, turn reference and capabilities come from the trusted backend context. Model arguments cannot supply a user ID or Pro entitlement. Updates and acknowledgement require a resource UUID; target resolution belongs to the orchestration layer.

The application adapter in `src/adapters/agent-core/legacy-reminders.ts` invokes the existing reminder executor and temporal parser. Creation accepts an explicit ISO instant; colloquial time interpretation remains a later integration. Recurrence, confirmation/nudge preferences and advance-notice behavior stay with the existing domain implementation and billing checks. The adapter preserves canonical cards, clarification questions and Pro CTA data for the later presenter.

Outcomes distinguish success, partial effects, clarification, denial, failure and indeterminate writes. A committed principal reminder survives an advance-notice failure as a partial result. Exceptions or malformed write results cannot claim success and are never retried by the adapter. Receipts correlate the user, operation, tool and returned resources; they are **not** a durable idempotency ledger. Persisted deduplication, reconciliation and approval enforcement remain required before wiring a multi-step loop to production.

Reads project allowed record fields into at most 50 structured records and at most five records in the model observation, with explicit bounded-list coverage. The full canonical card stays in presentation data. This bounds adapter output, not the database workload or production latency; query/index optimization remains a separate measured task.

Adapter tests exercise the current executor with mocked persistence: principal/advance ordering, past-time clarification without writes, partial save results, owned updates, acknowledgement and date scoping. They do not certify live Supabase behavior or model reasoning. A small runtime correction makes the existing read executor resolve “today” from the supplied turn reference instead of the server wall clock. The test deliberately sets those clocks to different years.

Two `.d.ts` files declare the narrow legacy executor and temporal-parser boundaries. They support temporary interoperability and do not count as converting those implementations to TypeScript. The complete reminder presentation/routing flow remains outstanding.

Verification on Node 24.15.0: strict type checking and package compilation passed; all 158 files / 1,910 tests passed in 38.72 seconds. The new reminder suites add 27 cases, including six against the existing executor with mocked persistence. No live provider or database calls were used. The inventory now contains 313 JS files and 16 TS files, including two transitional declarations; no legacy JS implementation has been retired in this increment. Machine-readable results are in `odd/evidence/agent-core-phase2/reminders.json`.

## Phase 2 task/calendar increment

The initial domain-tool scope is implemented and passes the offline contract/domain checks. The following eight tools join the four reminder tools:

| Tools | Behavior |
| --- | --- |
| `task_create`, `task_read` | One-task creation through the atomic batch API; bounded, owned reads with status/date/project filters. |
| `task_update` | Exact observed UUID; title, description/append, priority and due-date changes, including explicit null clears. |
| `task_complete`, `task_delete` | Exact observed UUID; existing reminder acknowledgement/cancellation cascades and partial receipts. Delete is classified as destructive and requires its own backend capability. |
| `calendar_read` | Bounded date scope, literal date or next event in the authenticated user's calendar. |
| `calendar_create` | Explicit timed interval or civil all-day dates with an exclusive end; optional Google Meet and timed WhatsApp companion. |
| `calendar_connect` | Verify existing connection or return the authorization CTA; generating a link does not mean authorization has completed. |

Calendar updates/deletion, disconnect, bulk mutations and broader domain coverage are not exposed by this initial tool set. They remain expansion work, consistent with Phase 5. Capability checks are the tool boundary; conversation approvals, persisted effect identity and duplicate/reconciliation handling must be integrated before admission to production.

Task creation currently confirms the persisted task but returns no receipt for its post-insert reminder. The adapter marks a requested future companion as unverified/partial. Calendar creation returns `reminderCreated`; an unconfirmed requested companion similarly yields a partial result while preserving the event receipt. The adapter does not retry either principal. Task update/complete/delete retain existing partial cleanup semantics and reject returned resource IDs that differ from the requested UUID.

Task flows retain their bounded, validated payload in transport/presentation data. Model observations omit flow tokens, raw owner metadata and task descriptions; the complete normalized result remains available to the host. Calendar records distinguish `timed` from `all_day`. A missing end date in an existing normalized all-day read stays null; the adapter does not fabricate it. Create inputs always require an explicit interval.

The task router now passes the turn reference through its read tool/service, and the calendar read passes it to the existing resolver. Without a reference, behavior still defaults to the current clock. Existing “today” semantics are preserved: tasks/calendar use the remaining portion of the day; reminder reads use their full-day policy. No scheduler or persistence schema changed.

Verification: 160 files / 1,952 tests passed in 37.00 seconds; 80 focused tests passed across five files; strict type checking and package compilation passed. The new task/calendar suites add 42 cases, including 11 actual executor paths with simulated persistence/Google Calendar. No live provider, Google or database calls were made. Results are in `odd/evidence/agent-core-phase2/tasks-calendar.json`. The inventory contains 313 JS files and 26 TS files, including two transitional declarations. No legacy JS implementations were retired; the new code is strict TS and the final conversion requirement still applies.

## Phase 3 isolated reminder flow

`ReminderAssistant.handle` connects normalized text, the provider port, typed reminder tools, the current domain executor and a deterministic reply. Voice transcripts can enter through the same text boundary; speech transcription and webhook integration are not part of this increment.

`WhatsAppCardPresenter` uses the existing canonical reminder formatters with normalized verified records. It combines creation cards and deduplicates records by identity, preserves recurrence and advance notices, distinguishes partial/unknown outcomes, retains clarification and separates HTTPS CTAs from text. Neither provider-authored prose nor the legacy `canonicalCard` field is rendered. This prevents the tested internal-instruction strings from reaching those cards; it is not a certification of every possible prompt-injection scenario. A no-tool response uses a deterministic reminder question; open-ended chat/tone adaptation remains with the existing production flow.

The canonical creation formatter now pairs advance notices by parent identity when available, keeping the legacy title fallback for rows without parent IDs. This prevents two same-title reminders from displaying each other's notice time. Eight checked snapshots cover the ES/EN visual patterns, aggregation, recurrence, partial/unknown results and the three-turn journey.

The runner makes one provider decision per turn, executes a bounded tool batch and records paired assistant calls/tool observations for the next turn. It namespaces provider-local call IDs, validates checkpoint ownership/history, retains observed references and blocks mutations of unobserved IDs. Backend role capabilities are checked by tools; writes additionally require a host authorization callback, denied by default. The host must derive that authorization from the user's request/approval and bind it to the intended action/resource. Provider permission prose does not authorize a write.

The initial Phase 3 runner supported one decision, bounded calls/output/history/deadline and explicit host authorization. Phase 4 below extends it while preserving the single-decision default and durable evidence boundaries.

`executionMode: 'shadow'` blocks all reminder writes before consulting host authorization, while permitting authorized reads. Proposed calls are available in the returned transcript for comparison. Use a separate shadow checkpoint and captured/isolated read results; do not overwrite the active production conversation with shadow state. No production shadow traffic is enabled.

The isolated journey tests three sequential inputs: create → reference update → acknowledgement, switching to English on the final turn. It uses scripted `MockAIProvider` decisions and the real existing executor with stateful simulated persistence. It verifies exactly one principal creation, one update of the same ID, one acknowledgement, current reference facts and valid tool history. Sequential replay is refused when the returned checkpoint is supplied. These tests validate orchestration and presentation, not live model understanding or WhatsApp delivery.

Verification: strict type checking/package compilation and compiled ESM exports passed; 163 files / 1,976 tests passed in 37.38 seconds, including 104 focused cases across eight files. The increment adds 24 cases. Results are in `odd/evidence/agent-core-phase3/reminder-flow.json`. Inventory: 313 JS files and 35 TS files, including three transitional declarations. No legacy JS implementation has been retired.

Before production admission, persist/fence effect identity, reconcile uncertain writes, serialize conversation updates and integrate idempotent outbound delivery. The in-checkpoint request guard is not crash recovery, distributed deduplication or a durable outbox. Live provider/persistence gates and router/transport parity remain outstanding; Phase 3 is not fully rollout-ready.

## Phase 3 durable reminder boundaries

`DurableReminderAssistant` now persists execution and delivery evidence through the existing inbound worker lease. It is an isolated composition: the production router still uses the existing assistant. The reminder scheduler remains unchanged. No new database schema, RPC or automatic recovery worker is introduced.

The inbound worker exposes its claimed run/message/thread identity and a copy of its prior response inside the existing async lease context. Durable admission requires that context, matching run/request/thread identities, and no prior `agentCore` checkpoint. A synchronous per-lease guard rejects concurrent entry. The existing database queue supplies cross-worker ownership and thread serialization; the new guard alone is not distributed deduplication. The host still authenticates the user, loads owned conversation state, authorizes writes and binds the delivery port to the recipient.

| Boundary | Persisted evidence | Failure behavior |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Before provider work | Version, owner, run and request identity | Stop before inference/effects if storage or lease fails. |
| Before each tool dispatch | Namespaced call ID and exact arguments | Stop before dispatch if the checkpoint fails; recheck deadline/cancellation afterward. |
| After each outcome | Normalized result and bounded observation | Propagate persistence failure; retain the pending call if its receipt cannot be stored. |
| Before outbound work | Completed conversation/UI/reply and per-message delivery plan | No send until the final result is persisted. |
| Before each send | Individual delivery ID and `attempted` state | No send if persistence/ownership fails. |
| After provider acceptance | Provider message ID and `accepted` state | Receipt-persistence failure leaves an uncertain attempt; never resend automatically. |

Every checkpoint replaces the complete `agentCore` object because the existing RPC merges only top-level JSON keys. It retains ordinary transport checkpoints under their existing keys. Cards and CTAs have separate delivery identities and receipts. Provider acceptance does not prove handset delivery. A transport exception or missing provider identity is conservatively stored as unknown, even when a host could later prove a rejection.

The runner's optional `ReminderExecutionJournal` keeps persistence outside tool-error catches. A storage failure cannot be converted into a routine tool observation followed by more execution. An indeterminate write blocks remaining writes in the batch while preserving valid tool history; reads may continue. The durable composition stores that outcome, sends the deterministic warning, then raises `EFFECT_RECONCILIATION_REQUIRED` so the existing worker quarantines the turn as `unknown`. Reconciliation is required before resuming an interrupted turn; automatic uncertain-effect or outbound replay is intentionally unavailable.

Verification: strict TypeScript/package compilation and compiled ESM imports passed. The full suite passed 164 files / 1,997 tests in 60.06 seconds; focused core and inbound-worker checks passed 130 cases across ten files in 5.00 seconds. This increment adds 21 cases covering checkpoint failures before/after writes, concurrent admission, identity mismatch, lease cancellation, uncertain writes, card/CTA receipts and interrupted sends. Persistence and transport are simulated; the actual worker lease bridge is exercised separately. No live database, model or WhatsApp calls were made. Evidence: `odd/evidence/agent-core-phase3/durable-reminder-flow.json`.

Inventory: 313 JS and 38 TS files, including four transitional declarations. The new durable coordinator and execution port are strict TS; existing worker/lease implementations remain JS pending their planned conversion. This does not complete the full-TS migration.

Before rollout, connect the typed checkpoint recovery port described below, engine pinning, the authenticated router and the real transport adapter; provide an authoritative reconciliation workflow for unknown turns/effects and verify it against real storage. Interrupted attempts are quarantined rather than recovered automatically. External sends have no new exactly-once guarantee. Live semantic/latency/cost and storage gates remain outstanding.

## Phase 3 owned checkpoint recovery and reconciliation inspection

The durable assistant can now load the previous conversation through `ReminderStateRecoveryPort` instead of receiving an optional checkpoint from its caller. When recovery is configured, caller-supplied overrides are rejected. The existing isolated mode remains available for fixtures; production admission must supply a trusted recovery implementation.

`createReminderStateRecovery` reads the existing service-only queue with three projected queries, each returning at most one row: the current running turn identity/sequence, the earliest older unknown turn, and the latest older terminal turn. Every query is scoped to the leased thread; prior queries require a lower sequence. Ownership and cancellation are checked before/after reads. Query errors, invalid returned identity/order, foreign users, corrupt checkpoints and unsupported versions stop admission rather than start an empty conversation. Empty state is allowed only when prior terminal history is absent.

The earliest-unknown lookup covers all prior turns in the thread, so a newer successful checkpoint cannot conceal an older uncertain effect. The policy conservatively blocks unknown deliveries as well as unknown domain operations. This is a thread-level fence, not a guarantee across multiple threads/accounts. No unresolved turn is marked resolved or automatically requeued.

`reminderCheckpointSchema` validates the persisted version, owner/request identity, complete tool transcript, matching recorded outcomes and final UI, unique effect/delivery identities and accepted provider receipts. A completed checkpoint must contain completed conversation state, verified outcomes and accepted deliveries. The durable writer uses the same schema before persisting. The recovered conversation retains observed reminder references and handled request IDs; fresh tool capabilities and host authorization still apply on the next turn.

`inspectReminderTurn` produces a reconciliation report with pending effect IDs, uncertain delivery IDs and whether complete conversation state exists. It does not replay tools/sends, infer a creation receipt from matching title/time, change stored status, or close reconciliation. Missing V2 state on the latest prior turn requires an explicit legacy handoff; it cannot be silently skipped to restore an older V2 checkpoint.

The cross-turn fixture stores the first turn's creation checkpoint, loads it through the trusted port and updates that exact reminder ID on the second turn, with one principal mutation and one delivery per turn. Recovery failures are tested before provider work, effects, current-turn checkpoints and delivery.

The structural SDK boundary keeps the core independent of Supabase authentication/browser declarations. Direct `SupabaseClient` type imports currently expose an SDK WebAuthn/DOM declaration incompatibility with the strict TypeScript 7 toolchain. No broad `skipLibCheck` or dependency change was introduced. The installed SDK was exercised with intercepted fetch: three thread-scoped GET queries, correct status/order/sequence/limit projections, zero network calls. `abortSignal` is applied before terminal `maybeSingle`; see the official [ordering documentation](https://supabase.com/docs/reference/javascript/order) and [sequence filter documentation](https://supabase.com/docs/reference/javascript/lt). Real database/query-plan/latency verification remains a pre-canary gate.

Verification: 165 files / 2,017 tests passed in 63.00 seconds; focused core/worker checks passed 150 cases across eleven files in 4.97 seconds. This increment adds 20 cases. Strict TypeScript/package compilation, compiled ESM exports and whitespace checks passed. Inventory: 313 JS / 41 TS files, including four transitional declarations; no legacy JS implementation was retired. Evidence: `odd/evidence/agent-core-phase3/checkpoint-recovery.json`. No production routing, schema, real provider/database/WhatsApp calls or dependency updates were introduced.

## Phase 3 authoritative reconciliation and explicit legacy handoff

`reconcileReminderTurn` closes a prior V2 unknown turn from trusted evidence without executing domain tools or sending messages. The host must supply a durable audit/provider evidence port and the server-assigned `agent_core.reconcile` capability; neither user/model prose nor a matching reminder title/time is evidence. Missing, foreign, mismatched or ambiguous receipts leave the turn blocked.

Write receipts bind owner, operation ID, canonical SHA-256 arguments fingerprint, exact tool and normalized terminal outcome. A committed write must include verified resource data; a no-effect receipt must explicitly establish `RECONCILED_NO_EFFECT`. A timeout/generic failure cannot establish absence of a write. Uncertain outbound attempts require independently verified accepted/not-sent evidence. Ready delivery plans have no attempted send and are abandoned rather than dispatched during reconciliation.

New V2 checkpoints store the initial owned conversation and normalized input before work. This enables reconstruction of a valid closed tool transcript when execution was interrupted before final state was stored. Successful receipts refresh observed references; verified no-effect outcomes add no resource. Existing complete conversation state can also be reconciled. Older incomplete checkpoints lacking both initial and completed state remain blocked for an explicit baseline; this adapter does not fabricate missing history. Unexecuted remaining batch actions and unsent replies are not resumed automatically.

`handoffLegacyReminderTurn` requires the separate server-assigned `agent_core.handoff` capability, a completed legacy queue turn, an accepted legacy delivery receipt, authenticated thread ownership and no active AgentRun. It extracts IDs from backend execution facts, deduplicates them and reloads each exact reminder under its user owner. Legacy cards, summaries and prose cannot create references. The resulting reminder conversation intentionally starts with empty model history and fresh owned reminder references; this boundary does not migrate all historical chat, task/calendar state, memory or pending approvals. Active runs remain with their existing engine.

`createLegacyReminderHandoffSource` supplies the exact-ID, owner-filtered projected database read; the host supplies its existing authenticated thread map and active-run lookup. Missing/deleted/foreign records block the handoff. The installed Supabase SDK query was checked through intercepted fetch with no outbound network call.

The generated additive migration `20261011173445_agent_core_turn_resolution.sql` defines service-only `resolve_hermes_prior_turn`. It requires the current running worker's lease/token, the same thread and a strictly older target. A full original-response comparison provides optimistic concurrency; changed evidence or a prior resolution rejects closure. Row locks serialize competing closures, and lease expiry is checked again after acquiring the target lock. The RPC preserves original execution/delivery evidence, adds `agentCoreResolution`, and closes unknown processing without requeueing or changing domain records. Recovery validates resolution ownership, original-source fingerprint and proof coverage before accepting the resolved conversation.

The SQL function enforces ownership/order/concurrency. Trusted evidence verification and strict resolution validation remain in the typed service; the service role already has privileged table access. Public, anonymous and authenticated clients have no execution permission. The current inbound readiness version remains unchanged because this optional function does not alter the legacy queue protocol. Connecting the new reconciliation path requires applying the migration and supplying its evidence/authorization sources. See the official [RPC documentation](https://supabase.com/docs/reference/javascript/rpc).

The migration and 15 SQL assertions passed on an isolated local PostgreSQL 17 database in a rolled-back transaction, including stale source, wrong/expired lease, foreign thread, future target, duplicate closure, evidence preservation, legacy handoff and role permissions. No production migration or domain/transport mutation was performed. The migration is prepared for review; real provider receipt integration, production storage gates and engine/router admission remain outstanding.

Verification: 166 files / 2,041 tests passed in 38.09 seconds; 174 focused tests passed across twelve files in 3.12 seconds, with 24 new cases. Strict type checking/package compilation, compiled ESM exports and whitespace checks passed. Inventory: 313 JS / 46 TS files, including four transitional declarations; no legacy JS implementation was retired. Evidence: `odd/evidence/agent-core-phase3/turn-resolution.json`. No live model, production database or WhatsApp calls were made.

The cohort selector is a pure function and does not enable v2 traffic. Authenticated identity and persisted engine version must come from trusted application state. Global disable affects new runs; active runs stay with their pinned engine. Durable routing/rollback integration remains future work.

## Baseline and migration inventory

- Baseline commit: `0a24b8d5bb3261063488cbe868419b8d9faa90f3`.
- Branch: `feat/templelabs-agent-core`.
- Working-tree baseline included preexisting TypeScript/tsx/Zod/Node-types dependency additions; those were preserved.
- Before implementation: 155 test files / 1,872 tests passed in 39.61 seconds locally on Node 24.15.0.
- After implementation: 156 test files / 1,883 tests passed; 11 new foundation cases passed with 7 ms of test execution in the focused run. The full-suite wall time was 68.53 seconds; this is not a provider or product latency measurement.
- The existing offline evaluation harness passed 50/50 smoke fixtures with outbound networking blocked. This is harness/fixture validation, not live model certification.
- Both workspace frozen-lockfile installation and a production-only installation with the Dockerfile's two-manifest context passed. Compiled ESM imports were checked locally; Node 22 checks are configured in CI, not claimed as locally executed.
- Initial source inventory: 313 JavaScript files and 7 TypeScript files across `src`, `scripts`, `tests`, `test` and `packages`; generated outputs are excluded.
- Existing local unit measurements include serialized request/schema sizes and mocked router timings. They are not production latency or provider token counts.

`migration:inventory` lists every remaining JS source in those maintained roots. Keep it current and retire the list incrementally; converting only `packages` does not meet the Full TS requirement. Worker conversion must preserve dispatch behavior. Extra legacy artifacts in `scratch`/`tmp` are not production source.

## Acceptance thresholds before production comparison

These are predeclared comparison rules, not achieved production measurements:

| Area | Gate |
| --- | --- |
| Existing correctness | No failures in required existing regression suites. |
| Provider quality | No regressions on the pinned required semantic fixtures; mock tests alone cannot satisfy this gate. |
| Simple-turn latency | Same-provider/model, equivalent-workload p95 no more than 10% above the measured baseline. |
| Simple-turn efficiency | Median total input/output cost per completed turn no higher than the measured baseline; target a reduction. |
| Complex work | Conditional multi-step scenarios complete correctly within configured step/call/time/token/spend limits. |
| Effects and identity | Zero unauthorized cross-user effects or replayed writes in targeted duplicate/concurrency/indeterminate-outcome cases. |
| Scheduling | No behavior regression or increased due-to-dispatch p95 under equivalent load; current worker polls every 15 seconds. |

Capture provider/model, fixture revision, environment, sample count and actual usage before judging those relative thresholds. Real latency/cost baselines, symptom reproductions and authorized live evaluation are still required before canary admission. No live LLM budget was consumed by this unit.

## Delivery boundary and next increment

CI now builds/types-checks the packages and tests, runs the existing regression command and verifies compiled ESM imports. Root workspace dependencies are development-only while v2 is disconnected; the production Dockerfile still starts the legacy application. TypeScript foundations are not evidence that v2 is deployment-ready.

Rollback of this unit removes `packages/`, the three root TS configs, the new tests/fixtures/inventory script and their workspace/scripts/CI entries. Preserve the dependency additions that predated this work. Generated output can be rebuilt; no database migration is introduced.

Continue Phase 5 gateway parity beyond the native and initial-foreground increments, and close the outstanding Phase 4 provider-aware tokenization and real-provider semantic evaluation gates. Mixed-domain presentation and durable completed-state recovery are implemented below. Mixed unknown-effect reconciliation and authenticated engine handoff remain separate prerequisites before activation. The reminder loop, context window and operational summaries are implemented below. Connect the reconciliation evidence source, authenticated legacy handoff guards, engine pinning and real transport only after their integration/storage gates pass. Keep the isolated read-date and parent-association corrections reviewable separately from the typed infrastructure. Offline checks pass for the implemented tool/presenter/durability/recovery/resolution scope; live gates and durable routing remain required before canary. The final destination remains full strict TypeScript.

The task/calendar increment can be removed independently by reverting its new contract/tool/adapter/test files, their exports and the widened executor declaration. The reference-clock correction is a separate four-file unit (`src/agent/tools/index.js`, `task-read.js`, `calendar.js` and `src/features/tasks/service.js`); retain or revert it with its targeted parity assertions. Neither unit introduces a database migration.

The Phase 3 unit consists of the turn/UI contracts, runner, presenter, assistant composition and their tests/snapshots, plus the narrow formatter declaration. Removing it restores the preceding package exports; the provider-contract extraction can remain as an independent refactor. The parent-association correction can also remain independently with its regression coverage. This unit adds no production route, sender or database migration.

The durable increment can be removed by reverting `durable-reminder-assistant.ts`, its tests, the optional runner journal/batch fence and corresponding presenter handling, plus the lease metadata/accessor/declaration and worker assertion. Existing queue SQL and legacy delivery behavior remain independently maintained; no migration rollback is needed.

The recovery increment consists of `reminder-checkpoint.ts` and its contract export, `reminder-state-recovery.ts`, the assistant's optional recovery/schema validation, and their tests/evidence. Reverting it restores the previous caller-supplied checkpoint boundary without changing existing queue SQL. The SDK verification harness is local, with simulated transport; it is not a live deployment or database migration.

The resolution increment includes the resolution contracts/fingerprint/reconciler/legacy source, checkpoint initial state, resolution-aware recovery, the optional worker/storage resolution bridge and its tests. The additive migration has not been deployed. Removing this increment leaves uncertain turns quarantined and legacy handoff blocked as before; it does not remove or change existing queue/effect evidence. If later deployed, disable this path before removing its service-only function.


## Phase 4 increment: bounded reminder continuation and context window

`ReminderTurnRunner` accepts `continuation: 'observation_driven'` to inspect observations and issue subsequent calls. Compatibility mode remains `single_decision` by default, avoiding another completion for existing simple turns. Defaults for observation mode are four model steps, four total tool calls, 15 seconds, 1,024 output tokens per completion and a 32,000-token cumulative reservation/usage ceiling. These are isolated defaults, not measured product targets. Final model prose still does not enter WhatsApp cards.

Each batch closes all assistant/tool pairs before continuation. IDs include turn and step identity. Reads expose the existing bounded normalized observations; their verified IDs permit a later update. Validation errors permit correction without dispatching invalid arguments. An identical successful or partial write, compared by sorted argument keys, is denied for the rest of the run. An indeterminate write stops continuation and retains the durable reconciliation requirement. This local duplicate guard does not replace backend idempotency or infer equivalence between differently expressed arguments.

Input reservation includes persona, verified reference facts, selected history and tool schemas. The default estimator conservatively uses serialized UTF-8 bytes plus a framing allowance; a host can inject a provider-specific estimator. Actual reported input/output usage accumulates across completions. A spend cap requires explicit input/output provider prices, reserves spend before calls and checks reported usage before dispatching effects. Returned `usage` reports model steps, token usage and the priced estimate, or null when prices are absent. It is runtime telemetry, projected out of the version-2 durable checkpoint. An inaccurate estimator or provider usage overrun cannot retroactively prevent billed inference; real tokenizer/framing checks remain required. Deadlines are checked at dispatch/continuation boundaries and passed to the provider; ports must honor cancellation/deadlines for a wall-clock guarantee.

`compactContext` runs when the message ceiling or estimated remaining token budget requires it. It retains the latest three complete user turns with their assistant/tool batches. It never changes durable history, verified reminder references or handled request IDs. Verified references remain structured system context; older user prose is not promoted into evidence. If the minimum recent window exceeds the message ceiling, admission fails; if essential context still exceeds token/spend limits, the runner stops rather than deleting it. The request ledger's existing 100-entry checkpoint limit remains explicit. Storage retention and cross-domain memory/approval integration remain future work; the reminder operational summary increment below preserves historical pending tool state.

Tests exercise read → update → final decision, argument correction, reordered duplicate writes, unknown writes, cumulative step/call/token limits, priced spend admission, deadlines, fatal journal failures and context preservation. A durable integration fixture checkpoints both model batches before the final decision and sends one response with an accepted receipt. Existing one-decision behavior remains covered.

This completes the first offline Phase 4 increment for reminders. The 1,800-token ordinary-input soft target, provider-specific tokenization, cross-domain conversational memory summaries, mixed-domain durable/presentation integration and real-model cost/latency/quality gates remain pending. No production routing, scheduler change, live inference or rollout is included. Remaining maintained JavaScript still needs gradual conversion to satisfy full strict TypeScript.

Rollback this increment by removing `context-manager.ts`, its core export and multihop tests; restoring the prior runner; and reverting the explicit runtime-result projection in the durable assistant and its multihop fixture. Existing Phase 3 storage and reconciliation artifacts remain independently maintained.

Validation for this increment: strict typecheck and compiled ESM exports passed; 12 focused files / 178 tests passed in 2.86 seconds; the full regression suite passed 167 files / 2,052 tests in 37.43 seconds. Inventory is 313 JS / 48 TS files, including four transitional declarations; this increment retired no legacy JS implementation. Evidence: `odd/evidence/agent-core-phase4/multihop-context.json`. Test wall time is not product or provider latency.


## Phase 4 increment: historical operational summaries

`prepareReminderContext` now tries complete turn boundaries, retaining the longest window that fits and at least the latest three user turns. When older messages leave the provider request, a version-1 `ReminderOperationalSummary` records their operational context from the host-owned tool transcript: pending approvals, clarifications, unknown actions, arguments, bounded clarification questions and an overview of the last five confirmed writes. Every entry retains its call identity. The summary declares historical provenance and `grantsAuthorization: false`; it never asserts a fresh database read. Free-form user/model/observation prose is not summarized into operational facts. Partial operations remain pending, including those without follow-up questions; new same-argument success does not infer reconciliation of prior partial effects.

A later fully successful call with identical normalized arguments supersedes an earlier approval or clarification in the descriptive summary. Partial success does not clear it. No later success infers resolution of an indeterminate effect; that requires the existing independent reconciliation path. The runner also rejects a prior indeterminate reminder history before provider admission, extending the durable recovery guard to isolated callers. Host write authorization remains mandatory for every action even if the summary shows a prior approval request or matching completion.

The estimate includes the summary, updated verified references, persona, history and schemas. `maxInputTokens` supplies a separate per-route input ceiling, checked before inference and against reported input usage before effects. The cumulative turn ceiling remains independent. A summary or essential recent window that exceeds token limits is retained and causes a limit result; it is never silently stripped to fit. The runner reserves an assistant-message slot and rejects oversized response batches before effects. If the next continuation no longer fits after a confirmed effect, it returns that paired receipt with `limit_reached`. Estimator errors and journal failures remain fatal.

Summaries are derived on demand and are not persisted as another source of truth. Version-2 checkpoints keep the complete source history, verified references and handled request ledger, so recovery and reconciliation use their existing evidence. This is deterministic reminder operational summarization with no extra model request. It does not import general conversation memories, prove model comprehension, replace provider tokenization, or satisfy the 1,800-token soft target by assertion. Real-provider quality/latency/cost gates and remaining domain integration are still pending.

This work unit consists of `packages/contracts/src/operational-context.ts`, `packages/agent-core/src/operational-context.ts`, their barrel exports, runner context/input-budget admission changes and `tests/unit/agent-core/operational-context.test.ts`. Reverting it restores the preceding whole-turn context manager while leaving durable storage/reconciliation and the bounded multihop loop intact. No migration or production wiring is added.

Validation for the operational-summary increment: strict typecheck passed; 13 focused files / 192 tests passed in 3.43 seconds; full regression passed 168 files / 2,066 tests in 40.00 seconds. A compiled-ESM fixture preserved its historical approval while reducing the conservative serialized-byte estimate from 19,436 to 8,368; these are fixture estimates, not measured model tokens. Inventory is 313 JS / 51 TS files, including four transitional declarations; no legacy JS implementation was retired. Evidence: `odd/evidence/agent-core-phase4/operational-context.json`.


## Phase 4 increment: one shared loop and multi-domain execution

`AgentLoop` now owns the common provider, budgets, steps, call identities, closed transcripts, capability-scoped tool admission, duplicate-write guards and dispatch boundaries. `ReminderTurnRunner` delegates to it while retaining its public result/journal API, single-decision default and version-2 durable checkpoint composition. Domain policies supply context, observed-reference checks, output normalization and journal hooks. The registry exposes tool effect metadata so reads and destructive actions are classified by their registered contract.

`ProductivityTurnRunner` composes the twelve atomic reminder/task/calendar tools over that shared loop. It returns discriminated typed domain facts and separate owned reference maps. Task update/complete/delete require an observed task ID; a deleted reference cannot be changed. Reads can run without a journal; mutations require both an explicit execution journal and host authorization. Missing capabilities block dispatch before the journal. Task/calendar results must match their operation, receipt resources and requested target. Reminder creation can include its principal and advance records. Model prose is discarded. OAuth authorization-required results retain their CTA and do not imply a connected calendar; all-day dates retain their civil exclusive end.

Cross-domain scenarios now cover task read → update/complete, calendar read → create and calendar read → task create → reminder create. Actual current task/calendar executors run behind simulated database/Google ports in the integration fixtures, retaining owner filters, reference clock and linked-reminder completion. These scripted provider tests verify protocol/data flow, not that a real model chooses the correct conditional action. Bounded calendar results are not a proof of slot availability; the host must still validate scheduling intent and any required preconditions.

A lost write receipt stops all following writes and continuation. A confirmed task/event with an unverified companion reminder remains `partial`, preserves the principal evidence and sets `requiresReconciliation`; following writes are blocked and a later turn is rejected until independent reconciliation changes the verified source state. This avoids retrying a confirmed principal to repair an uncertain companion. Fully known companion cleanup failures can remain partial facts; the identical principal write is still blocked within the run. Operational summaries now cover all three domains and keep historical partial pending state without inferring repair from a later same-argument success.

The new journal interface defines the persistence boundary; this increment does not supply a production mixed-domain storage implementation. Simulated journal fixtures prove dispatch/outcome ordering and fatal failures. `DurableReminderAssistant`, its reminder presenter, recovery/reconciler and version-2 SQL closure remain reminder-only. They must not accept a `ProductivityConversation` or reinterpret mixed effects as reminder checkpoints. Mixed-domain presentation, durable state/recovery/reconciliation and real delivery need their own tested composition before admission. The new runner is disconnected from the production router.

This unit includes `agent-loop.ts`, `productivity-turn.ts`, their exports, the discriminated productivity contracts, shared operational-summary extension, registry effect metadata, the reminder delegation and corresponding domain/legacy parity tests. Reverting it restores the prior reminder loop and summaries; retain the earlier reminder durability artifacts. No storage migration, scheduler change, dependency addition or production route is introduced. Existing compatibility checks and the full maintained-source TypeScript destination remain required.

Validation for this shared-loop increment: strict typecheck passed; 14 focused files / 216 tests passed in 4.01 seconds; full regression passed 169 files / 2,090 tests in 42.65 seconds. A compiled-ESM fixture completed task read → complete → final with intent/execution/observation ordering and valid transcripts. All storage/provider ports were simulated. Inventory is 313 JS / 55 TS files, including four transitional declarations; no legacy JS implementation was retired. Evidence: `odd/evidence/agent-core-phase4/productivity-loop.json`. Test/runtime fixture timing does not establish provider latency, cost or semantic quality.


## Phase 4 increment: deterministic mixed replies and durable continuity

An isolated productivity turn now has a complete typed path from multihop execution to deterministic WhatsApp presentation, checkpointed delivery and recovery. `WhatsAppProductivityPresenter` renders owned task/reminder/calendar facts and preserves HTTPS authorization/Pro CTAs and validated task flows. It reuses the existing canonical task/reminder formatters; calendar lines preserve the current vocabulary, civil all-day dates and safe Meet links. Cards deduplicate records within the same operation/read scope, while distinct reads remain distinct. Lists disclose bounded coverage, partial principal effects keep their confirmed cards plus a warning, and provider/canonical prose is excluded. Validated read scope travels with the outcome; successful outcomes must have complete evidence.

`DurableProductivityAssistant` requires the current inbound thread lease and persists the initial conversation, each dispatch intent, every outcome before continuation, the final reply and the complete text/CTA/flow delivery plan. Each send first becomes `attempted`; a nonempty provider acceptance ID must then be checkpointed. Acceptance does not certify handset delivery. An interrupted send or failed receipt checkpoint leaves `unknown`/`attempted` evidence and cannot replay automatically. Unknown primary effects or requested companion effects trigger an honest reply followed by queue quarantine.

| Boundary | Implemented behavior |
| --- | --- |
| Storage | Strict productivity checkpoint version 1 under `agentCoreProductivity`; reminder version 2 remains under `agentCore`. Final UI must match ordered journal outcomes; delivery content/IDs must match the final reply. |
| Admission | Both coordinators share synchronous lease admission. Matching run/message/thread identities are required, and either existing checkpoint namespace blocks a second execution. |
| Recovery | Shared projected queue reads verify the current lease/sequence, search all older unknown turns and then read the latest completed state. Completed mixed checkpoints restore all three reference maps, full closed history and handled request IDs. |
| Version isolation | Mixed recovery rejects reminder/legacy state pending explicit handoff. Reminder recovery, reconciliation, legacy handoff and the pending service-only SQL resolution function reject mixed state. |
| Remaining integration | Mixed unknown effects/deliveries require a future independent domain/provider evidence resolver. Authenticated engine handoff, actual sender/router binding and provider evaluation remain required before activation. |

The prepared, undeployed resolution migration adds a namespace rejection; no mixed-resolution SQL endpoint is claimed. The actual PostgreSQL fixture passed 17 assertions in the dedicated local Hermes test database, including both mixed-state rejection paths, ownership, ordering, optimistic response checks and role permissions. Queue schema/function/roles/fixture writes were inside one transaction and rolled back. Parallel-connection locking behavior and a live Supabase SDK round trip were not tested in this increment.

This unit adds the mixed presenter, checkpoint contract, coordinator, recovery adapter, shared queue reader/admission helper and corresponding fixtures/tests. The two additional formatter declarations are temporary JS interoperability, not converted implementations. For rollback, disable mixed admission first and retain checkpoint namespace/unknown-state fences wherever mixed state may have been persisted. Never reinterpret an interrupted mixed checkpoint as legacy reminder state; use a verified handoff/resolution before retiring its version. The production router is still disconnected, so this increment does not alter active product traffic.

Validation: strict typecheck and package compilation passed; 17 focused files / 254 tests passed in 5.94 seconds; the full suite passed 172 files / 2,128 tests in 54.46 seconds, adding 38 cases. A temporary compiled ESM application build exercised task read → complete → final → separately accepted text/flow → owned recovery using the actual coordinator and lease bridge with simulated domain/storage/provider/transport ports. Inventory is 313 JS / 67 TS files, including six transitional declarations; no legacy JS implementation was retired. Evidence: `odd/evidence/agent-core-phase4/productivity-durability.json`. No production database, real-model or WhatsApp calls, dependency changes, deployment or commits were made in this work unit.

Phase 5 begins below with the configured native OpenRouter route adapter. Continue the gateway migration by covering strict foreground decisions, background purposes and multimodal requests, with explicit compatibility checks. Use those adapters to close Phase 4 model quality/token/cost/latency gates, then continue the maintained JS inventory toward full strict TypeScript. Phase 4 is not certified complete merely because its offline orchestration checks pass.


## Phase 5 increment: native provider protocol and accountable fallback

`OpenRouterProvider` implements the neutral `AIProvider` interface for native atomic tool calls. `createConfiguredAIProvider` snapshots the existing gateway's configured primary/secondary/last-resort **native-tool** routes, timeouts and reasoning settings; package code does not read environment variables or credentials. Explicit provider `order`/`only`, `allow_fallbacks: false` and `require_parameters: true` match the current wire builder. Native tool pairs translate together with short provider-facing IDs; host operation IDs and resource arguments retain their own identities. Tool arguments must be object JSON, batches must have unique IDs and valid finish reasons, and a parseable truncated batch never becomes executable evidence. Refusals are terminal and discard internal prose.

The implementation uses the documented [OpenRouter native tool protocol](https://openrouter.ai/docs/guides/features/tool-calling) and [provider routing controls](https://openrouter.ai/docs/guides/routing/provider-selection). The native-only route configuration declares `structuredOutput: false`: this increment does not implement the existing strict `execute_turn` JSON-schema route, or assert that every configured model supports strict schemas.

| Boundary | Implemented behavior |
| --- | --- |
| Turn scope | The shared loop calls optional `AIProvider.forTurn()` once per run. A failed route cannot be revisited during later model steps; independent turns start a fresh scope. Configured route provider IDs must be distinct. |
| Cancellation | Host cancellation and the remaining whole-turn/route timeout cover fetch and response-body reading. A promise race bounds adapters that ignore the signal, with a final signal check before queued transport starts. This bounds local waiting, not remote billing cancellation. |
| Token budget | Metered failed attempts count toward the returned aggregate. Every fallback checks remaining input/total reservations and shares the output allowance. A route requiring more output than is available is skipped; the adapter never raises the caller cap to the legacy 2,800-token minimum. |
| Spend budget | The loop passes its remaining optional priced budget. Each route reserves a new conservative input estimate and output allowance against host-supplied rate ceilings, then checks reported usage before a tool decision can return. The host must provide ceilings covering **all** configured routes. No live prices or actual dollar-cost certification are inferred. |
| Missing evidence | Missing/invalid usage, unreadable bodies, network errors or timed-out attempts stop before fallback/effects; unknown inference spend is not silently replaced by zero. |
| Failure accounting | Vendor-neutral `AIProviderError` carries immutable known usage and `accountingComplete`. The loop retains failed-attempt counts, marks unknown accounting and returns `estimatedCostUsd: null` when complete accounting is unavailable. Previously confirmed effects remain visible and are not repeated. |

Successful non-streaming usage is normalized from `prompt_tokens`/`completion_tokens` according to the [OpenRouter API reference](https://openrouter.ai/docs/api_reference/overview). Error responses are not assumed to include that evidence. Consequently the new isolated adapter is **more restrictive than the legacy fallback chain** when accounting is unavailable; it does not claim full outage-availability parity. A trusted usage/billing reconciliation source or an explicitly bounded, evaluated alternative policy remains necessary before replacing the active gateway. Known metered malformed decisions may use an independent next route before effects, while authentication failures and normalized refusals terminate.

The gateway emits safe failure codes and does not log prompts, raw provider errors, headers or keys. Its key is an ECMAScript private field and cannot be exposed by ordinary object JSON serialization. The compiled runtime fixture compares the new request body to the existing pure wire builder (with explicit non-streaming), then exercises metered primary failure → secondary task read → complete → final → separately accepted text/flow → owned checkpoint recovery. Provider, domain, database and transport responses are simulated; this verifies protocol and boundaries, not model intelligence or production latency.

This review unit includes `packages/ai/src/openrouter-protocol.ts`, `openrouter-provider.ts`, `provider-error.ts`, the configured application adapter, the temporary production-client configuration declaration, optional request/scope/error-accounting integration in the shared loop, and tests. No dependencies, deployment, active router behavior, scheduler logic or schema migrations change. The active JS gateway still serves production. Reverting this adapter and its loop integration leaves the preceding isolated productivity flow intact; keep the earlier mixed-checkpoint version fences.

Validation: strict typecheck and package compilation passed; 19 focused files / 291 tests passed in 5.21 seconds; the full suite passed 174 files / 2,165 tests in 39.93 seconds, adding 37 cases. The compiled ESM fixture passed native wire parity, four metered HTTP attempts across three model steps (scripted aggregate 95 input / 32 output tokens), durable text/flow acceptance and owned recovery. Inventory is 313 JS / 74 TS files, including seven transitional declarations; this unit retired no legacy JS implementation. Evidence: `odd/evidence/agent-core-phase5/native-provider-gateway.json`. No live model, production database or WhatsApp requests were made. Node 24.15.0 was verified; configured Node 22 CI was not executed locally.

Phase 5 remains in progress. Next cover the current strict foreground protocol and background/multimodal purposes, then evaluate configured alternative adapters against the same semantic fixtures. Continue migrating the remaining application/storage/workers/tests/scripts and retire temporary declarations only alongside their TS implementations and runtime build paths. The destination is still full strict TypeScript, with live Phase 4 quality/token/cost/latency gates and mixed reconciliation/handoff prerequisites outstanding before canary.


## Phase 5 increment: portable structured output and initial foreground parity

Hermes now has a vendor-neutral `StructuredAIProvider` beside native tool completion. The request supplies a JSON Schema and a mandatory pure synchronous host validator; the result distinguishes validated JSON from refusal and carries normalized usage. A replacement provider can implement that contract without importing the OpenRouter adapter. Fixtures exercise an independent simulated provider and two configured model/provider identities through the same local business rules. They prove interchangeability of the contract, not comparative model quality or price.

`OpenRouterProvider` uses one bounded generation implementation for native and strict JSON modes, retaining cancellation, failed-route scope, verified usage aggregation and input/output/total/priced reservations. Strict mode requests `response_format: json_schema` with `strict: true` and non-streaming output, and emits no native tools or `tool_choice`. That omission matches the existing wire builder and avoids requiring an unrelated tool parameter from a strict-only endpoint. Strict routes need explicit declared support and incompatible routes are skipped. These declarations are configuration, not a live capability certification.

The structured decoder accepts a complete `stop` response, parses object JSON without prose cleanup/repair, then invokes the local validator. Literal whitespace and `<think>` strings inside JSON remain unchanged. Parseable truncated output, markdown/prefaced JSON, native calls in a strict response and malformed local results are rejected. Refusal is terminal. Validators returning a Promise or a non-plain object cannot masquerade as accepted data. The gateway rechecks time and cancellation after validation before returning success. Exact JSON Schema enforcement is supplied by the caller's local validator; this adapter does not implement a general JSON Schema validator or assume that remote enforcement alone grants authority. [OpenRouter documents endpoint-specific support and differing strict enforcement](https://openrouter.ai/docs/guides/features/structured-outputs).

`createConfiguredStructuredAIProvider` snapshots the current **strict** primary/secondary/last-resort settings, preserving their separate models/providers, reasoning and timeouts. The strict path does not inherit the native fallback's 2,800-token minimum. `completeForegroundDecision` reuses the current strict provider projection, canonical operation projection, domain validation and current-user profile-source rule. The authenticated host supplies admin authority and owned-history availability. Before projection, qualified operations must belong to the schema's actual allowlist; raw mixed `domain`/`action` representations are rejected. Attachment-only decisions cannot escape their conversation-only operation scope or introduce profile/learning metadata. After the provider returns, the host independently rechecks status/usage and canonical domain/source/operation scope. A future adapter that ignores the eager validation callback cannot bypass authority. These are local guards even if an endpoint ignores its schema.

Current foreground prerequisites remain intact: task listings need explicit status, and task creation declares either a project/entity or `projectless: true`. Initial integration fixtures were corrected to satisfy those rules. A foreign source quote on an otherwise valid task creation specifically fails `INVALID_TURN_CONTEXT`; the migration does not relax domain or evidence validation to make fixtures pass. Request schemas/messages/validators and host policy are captured before asynchronous fallback. Structured decisions are not automatically converted into atomic tool writes or sent to WhatsApp.

Validation: strict TypeScript/package compilation passed; 20 focused files / 322 tests passed in 4.81 seconds; the final full regression after the host-boundary correction passed 175 files / 2,196 tests in 109.40 seconds, adding 31 cases. A compiled ESM fixture using the actual configured strict adapter, legacy schema/validator and wire builder passed invalid task status → strict fallback → canonical `task.list` → next decision on the surviving route, with exact request-body parity. All three HTTP responses were simulated. Inventory is 313 JS / 78 TS files, including nine transitional declarations; no legacy JS implementation was retired. Evidence: `odd/evidence/agent-core-phase5/structured-provider.json`.

This unit includes the portable structured port/types, shared native/structured generation logic, strict codec and configuration factory, the pure initial foreground adapter, two temporary contract declarations and tests. The active JS gateway/router and scheduler remain unchanged; no dependencies, schema migrations, live provider/database/WhatsApp calls, deployment or commits were introduced. Remove this strict extension and initial-foreground adapter to return to the prior isolated native gateway; retain mixed-checkpoint fences and existing durable state.

Next adapt the approval/continuation decision wrappers, then background purposes and multimodal inputs; configured alternative providers need the same offline and real semantic fixtures. Missing accounting still stops fallback conservatively, so full outage-availability/billing parity remains open. The remaining maintained JS inventory, supported runtime build path and declaration retirement are still required for the full strict TypeScript destination. A future cheaper model is eligible after capability, semantic, latency and measured cost checks pass; this work makes changing that choice an adapter/configuration task.

## Phase 5 increment: portable approval and continuation decisions

`completeApprovalDecision` and `completeContinuationDecision` now use the same vendor-neutral structured provider contract. They reuse the actual legacy provider schemas and canonical validators, preserving language/profile rules, selective approval and per-action continuation deltas. Immediate schedules retain `relative_offset` with zero minutes. Approval suggestions include only currently prepared actions; executed/foreign/duplicate IDs and non-string selections are rejected. Continuations require nonempty canonical deltas for exact pending IDs; shared deltas are excluded from the new wire contract. Cancel, unrelated and supersede decisions discard action suggestions.

The authenticated caller supplies a suspended-run snapshot. The host verifies its owner, thread, waiting status and expiry before calling the model, captures its complete payload/targets/statuses and identifiers, and sends that snapshot as data alongside the request. Returned decisions carry a SHA-256 snapshot fingerprint, the suspended and inbound identities, `freshness: snapshot` and `grantsExecution: false`. This is a planning result: it does not approve, claim, merge or execute a stored action. The future runtime bridge must re-read the owned run, compare the current proposal, check authorization/status/expiry and atomically claim it before effects. The fingerprint alone is not a database revision or CAS guarantee.

The model deadline is capped at run expiry. Host time, cancellation and deadline are checked again after completion, including when an alternative adapter ignores the eager validation callback or its own timeout. Final host validation independently rejects invalid pending references and profile quotes absent from the current user message. Deeply detached drafts and captured identities/deadlines prevent caller mutation during asynchronous inference from changing what was reviewed. Refusal produces no suggestions. The configured OpenRouter route can fall back on a metered invalid selection before effects and aggregates usage across those attempts.

This unit includes the active-run contracts, two pure decision wrappers, their tests and one temporary declaration for the existing JS validators. Production still uses its existing approval/continuation executor and gateway; no router activation, storage migration, scheduler change, dependency addition or execution behavior is introduced. Rollback removes the isolated wrappers/contracts/declaration and retains the preceding portable gateway and durable checkpoint fences.

Validation: strict TypeScript/package compilation passed; 21 focused files / 360 tests passed in 7.18 seconds; the full regression passed 176 files / 2,234 tests in 45.94 seconds, adding 38 cases. A compiled ESM fixture using the actual configured adapter, legacy decision schemas/validators and wire builder passed executed-ID rejection → metered fallback → pending subset → per-action immediate continuation on the surviving route, with exact wire parity. Three HTTP responses were simulated; no effects or live provider/database/WhatsApp calls occurred. Inventory is 313 JS / 82 TS files, including ten transitional declarations; no legacy JS implementation was retired. Evidence: `odd/evidence/agent-core-phase5/active-run-decisions.json`.

Next cover background purposes and multimodal inputs, then the active-run execution/storage bridge and configured alternative providers. The full remaining JS-to-TS migration and live semantic/cost/latency checks remain required; this increment certifies local decision boundaries only.

## Phase 5 increment: auxiliary jobs and image/PDF evidence

The portable gateway now separates `TextAIProvider` for auxiliary text/array output from `MediaAIProvider` for inline image/PDF evidence. Both require a pure local validator and distinguish complete text from refusal. Binary input is ephemeral and does not extend the persisted agent transcript or grant tool authority. Shared generation retains cancellation/deadlines, verified aggregate usage, host budgets and independent-route fallback. Auxiliary text rejects truncated completions and unsolicited tool calls before accepting output. A fresh provider scope per job/attachment prevents a failed route in one job from suppressing primary on later jobs.

`createBackgroundCompletion` can be injected into the current pure dispatch-policy compiler, memory-admission validator and next-best-action selector. Their prompts, schemas, semantic selection and downstream reducers remain the source of behavior; eager and final host checks reuse their actual validators. Policy/admission retain two strict routes with at most three seconds per route; optional advice uses only primary with two seconds. Host deadlines can be shorter, and output limits cannot exceed the job ceiling. Unknown spend stops fallback. No background worker or scheduler is activated or rewritten here.

`createMemoryExtractionCompletion` uses the separate current memory route configuration and accepts complete, bounded JSON arrays as proposals. It does not save memories or assert their grounding. Current trusted-user filtering, candidate normalization, semantic admission and storage checks must still run before persistence. This port is prepared but not injected into the active memory service. Plain text/JSON-object output is also available at the provider boundary; response-synthesis integration remains pending.

`extractAttachmentEvidence` reuses the existing extraction prompt via its newly exported pure builder. The result explicitly carries `provenance: untrusted_attachment` and `grantsExecution: false`. Trusted captions remain separate instructions for extraction; text visible inside an attachment remains evidence. Final host checks reject empty, late, aborted or malformed vendor results even if an alternative adapter ignores eager validation. Inline base64 supports JPEG/PNG/WebP/GIF and PDF, with a decoded 20 MiB ceiling and safe filenames; caller-selected remote URLs and unsupported formats are rejected before transport. A flat base64 scan avoids the stack overflow exposed by the large-input boundary test.

Configured media routes preserve the current vision/document model choices, timeouts, reasoning and output caps (800/700 tokens), with explicit provider pins. Images use text-first `image_url` parts; PDFs use `file` parts and explicitly request the native parser. These formats follow the [image input documentation](https://openrouter.ai/docs/guides/overview/multimodal/image-understanding) and [PDF documentation](https://openrouter.ai/docs/guides/overview/multimodal/pdfs). The latter documents optional OCR charges, so the isolated adapter excludes automatic OCR and implicit provider fallback. This deliberately differs from the legacy document fallback and needs live capability/availability evaluation before replacement. Route capability declarations and serialized-byte reservations do not certify image/page tokenization, factual extraction or measured cost.

This unit adds the media contract, portable text/media ports and codecs, purpose/media configuration, injected completion/evidence helpers, tests and one temporary prompt declaration. The existing document processor's only executable change is exporting its unchanged prompt builder. Production gateways, background workers, media processing, voice transcription, embeddings, scheduler and router continue using their current paths. Rollback removes the isolated extensions and the added export from the prompt builder; preserve previous native/structured gateways and durable-state fences.

No effects or live provider/database/WhatsApp calls occurred. Inventory is 313 JS / 87 TS files, including eleven transitional declarations; no legacy JS implementation was retired. ## Phase 5 increment: audio, embedding spaces and optional synthesis

`TranscriptionProvider` and `EmbeddingProvider` are now independent typed ports beside chat completion. A shared endpoint primitive bounds the single fetch and body decode, including transports that ignore cancellation. Deadlines and cancellation are rechecked before accepting results. Keys remain private, errors expose safe codes and reported accounting, and no raw transcript/provider error is logged by the new adapters.

The configured transcription factory explicitly selects either the current Groq multipart route (five seconds) or OpenRouter JSON route (six seconds). Groq keeps its vocabulary hint; OpenRouter keeps raw base64 plus format/language/temperature. `auto` omits a forced language in both. Byte snapshots are detached and bounded to 20 MiB; format metadata does not validate the actual audio codec, so ingestion must still verify it. `transcribeUserAudio` checks returned text and accounting independently of the vendor and grants no execution authority. Reported audio accounting distinguishes USD, seconds and optional token counts; missing accounting remains `unavailable`. Known audio cost survives late/invalid responses. This follows the [Groq transcription contract](https://console.groq.com/docs/speech-to-text) and [OpenRouter STT contract](https://openrouter.ai/docs/guides/overview/multimodal/stt); OpenRouter's STT provider `order`/`only` controls are not applied, so this adapter does not claim chat-style provider pinning. A duration-based spend reservation and evaluated retry policy remain necessary: no automatic multi-endpoint fallback is introduced into the isolated path. Active legacy voice fallback remains unchanged.

Embeddings retain the current scalar/batch wire shape, trimmed text and 768 dimensions. The new port binds a versioned space identity, model and dimensions. Responses must declare the expected model, contain exactly one finite vector per input and have unique, complete indexes; shuffled batches are restored to input order. Missing prompt usage fails closed; known usage remains visible on invalid/late output. Input count/size and optional token reservations bound requests. No model fallback can silently mix spaces. The wire and usage fields follow the [embedding API reference](https://openrouter.ai/docs/api/api-reference/embeddings/submit-an-embedding-request).

`MemoryEmbeddingClient` additionally enforces the declared current space at the final host boundary. Its explicit owner scope caches only query vectors, coalesces equal pending queries, removes failures, caps entries at 32 and returns detached copies. A reused result is marked as reused rather than represented as a new zero-cost inference. Foreign owners, unscoped calls, save-style calls and batches bypass query reuse. Enqueued work captures only its query scope, including an absent scope; no execution lease or database authority is captured. This is a separate prepared scope API and is not wired into the existing worker/database path. Existing stored rows have not been certified by a new persisted space-version migration. Changing the embedding model requires provenance/index evaluation and reindexing or a separately compatible index even when dimensions remain equal.

Optional synthesis reuses the actual legacy prompt and normalization through two extracted pure helpers. It keeps the JSON-object protocol, native route identities, low reasoning, temperature 0.2, maximum 180 output tokens and a whitespace-compacted prefix of at most 160 characters. The suffix stays empty, including the existing `after`-to-`before` fallback. Empty fact/exclusion inputs skip inference. The host checks returned JSON, usage and deadline even if a future adapter skips eager validation. These guards constrain format and authority; they do not certify the semantics of generated prose. The deterministic presenter still owns operational cards, and the new synthesis result is not automatically composed or sent.
