## Phase 5 increment: owned approval/resume execution bridge

`ActiveRunExecutionBridge` now connects the portable approval/continuation decisions to the existing domain executor through an isolated, host-owned persistence seam. Planning must first use `projectSuspendedAgentRun`: the execution-ready snapshot includes the run kind, waiting fields, temporal context and prior execution receipts alongside all target/payload/status fields. Those fields affect execution and belong in the reviewed proposal fingerprint. Earlier minimal planning snapshots remain accepted for planning but cannot grant execution. Canonical decision/source rules are rechecked at the host, and authenticated run/request/user/thread identities must match the current inbound lease. Unknown/failed prior actions and inconsistent domain/kind metadata cannot proceed.

The prepared migration adds backend-only, security-invoker RPC functions with an empty search path. `claim_hermes_active_run` locks the current inbound lease, the owned pending run and ordered actions, then compares the complete current proposal before changing status. It also rejects expired ownership, prior unknown turns, foreign engine checkpoints and repeated claims. Status change, a private claim nonce and the initial `agentCoreActiveRun` checkpoint are stored in the same transaction. A missing/changed proposal returns no grant; no process-memory fallback is available. PostgreSQL RPC/security behavior follows the [database-function documentation](https://supabase.com/docs/guides/database/functions).

`mutate_hermes_active_run` requires that same nonce and current live inbound lease for later persistence. Prepared-action updates compare the complete prior action before recording a fence or outcome. Terminal status cannot silently ignore added/removed actions, and terminal state cannot dispatch another effect. Final receipt retirement removes the transient claim from run context, allowing a genuinely still-pending clarification to be planned from a fresh snapshot. Action fences/outcomes are also retained in the inbound checkpoint. This is a lease-owned backend grant, not authority encoded in a model message.

The existing approval/resume executor still owns domain-call construction, date inheritance, validation, confirmed facts and canonical cards. Its optional injected runtime leaves the default legacy route intact; default getters preserve live storage bindings and existing failure-injection behavior. Approval suggestions expand to pending actions only, without replaying or overwriting previously executed actions. Continuation deltas remain per action; cancellation changes pending bookkeeping and performs no external tool effect. Authenticated Pro/admin flags come from the host. Before every write, the bridge verifies the live grant and host authorization, persists an unknown-outcome fence, then rechecks ownership before dispatch. Returned storage receipts must match the exact fence/outcome and owner/target/payload. Unknown effects stop subsequent writes and require reconciliation. Errors after a claim retain durable evidence and do not automatically reopen/replay the run, including a denied authorization or interrupted receipt.

The new checkpoint namespace is rejected by the reminder and mixed-engine recovery/admission paths and by the prepared reminder-only resolution function. It cannot masquerade as an old reminder/legacy handoff. This block returns verified execution facts; it does not supply active-run unknown-effect resolution, authenticated cross-engine history handoff, WhatsApp delivery or router activation. Those remain required before admitting traffic. No migration was applied to production or recorded as a production migration history entry.

Validation: project strict TypeScript/package compilation passed; 24 focused files / 451 tests passed in 7.81 seconds; the final full regression after the default-storage compatibility correction passed 179 files / 2,325 tests in 44.70 seconds, adding 26 cases. Actual PostgreSQL passed 33 active-run assertions plus 19 reminder-resolution assertions inside one rolled-back transaction in the dedicated local Hermes test database. A separate newly created local database exercised two real PostgreSQL sessions: the duplicate waited on the owner lock, exactly one claim succeeded and the start checkpoint was atomic. That database was removed afterward. A compiled ESM fixture exercised actual structured decision wrappers, RPC adapter, bridge and legacy executor for approval and immediate continuation with simulated provider/storage/tools; authorization and durable fences preceded both simulated effects.

The temporary compiled legacy-JS fixture uses `skipLibCheck: true` because importing the existing Supabase auth SDK exposes a WebAuthn declaration conflict under TypeScript 7. Project/package strict checks remain enabled and passed; resolving that dependency conflict is still a full-TypeScript runtime-build gate. The fixture proves runtime interoperability, not a fully strict emitted legacy application or live Supabase SDK round trip. Inventory is 313 JS / 99 TS files, including twelve transitional declarations; no legacy JS implementation was retired. Evidence: `odd/evidence/agent-core-phase5/active-run-execution.json`.

This unit includes the expanded snapshot, typed RPC/store/bridge, one temporary executor declaration, the optional legacy runtime seam, namespace fences, prepared SQL and tests. No dependencies, scheduler behavior, active router path, live provider/production database/WhatsApp calls, deployment or commits changed. Rollback can remove the disconnected bridge and optional seam. If its checkpoint or claim has ever been persisted, first disable admission and retain the namespace/claim fences and evidence until verified resolution/handoff; never restore the old executor over an interrupted grant.

Next close active-run recovery/reconciliation and authenticated engine handoff, then continue the maintained application/storage/workers/scripts/tests conversion and retire declarations alongside implementations. Independent provider evaluation, real semantic/cost/latency gates and the other pending Phase 5/6 rollout prerequisites remain open. The full strict TypeScript destination is unchanged.

## Phase 5 increment: active-run recovery evidence reader

An interrupted execution can have a persisted action receipt without a final turn receipt. The new strict `activeRunCheckpointSchema` describes both claimed and final checkpoints, including the original complete proposal and each action receipt. `inspectActiveRunRecovery` compares that evidence with the owned current run/actions, validates immutable identities, the claim marker, context, final status and exact expiry instant. Timestamp comparison preserves PostgreSQL microseconds across equivalent UTC offsets. It retains the entire original inbound response fingerprint rather than replacing historical metadata.

`createActiveRunRecoveryReader` requires the authenticated reconciliation capability and the current ambient inbound lease. Its private RPC owner token never becomes model input. The prepared `read_hermes_active_run_recovery` function locks the current owner, chooses the oldest prior unknown turn before considering the latest completed turn, then reads the matching owned run and ordered actions. The returned anchor binds the current request/thread/sequence; the host checks it independently before returning evidence. Foreign or malformed namespaces return unavailable, while only an explicit absence marker means no prior turn exists. Cancellation and a fifteen-second RPC deadline remain enforced even for an uncooperative transport. There is no storage fallback. The private security-invoker function follows the [Supabase database-function guidance](https://supabase.com/docs/guides/database/functions); the prepared partial thread/sequence index covers completed and unknown history.

The report distinguishes pending and skipped calls, unknown fences and actions requiring independent outcome verification. Executed/failed actions and every non-null fact still require verification; a legacy success receipt or final reply cannot certify the external effect. Delivery always requires separate verification. Reports explicitly carry `grantsExecution: false` and `allowsHandoff: false`. Expiration does not erase historical evidence. This reader performs no tool dispatch, send, reopening, resolution write or transfer to the mixed engine. Final evidence-based CAS closure and authenticated history/reference handoff remain the next increment.

Validation: strict project/package checks passed; 25 focused files / 482 tests passed in 20.72 seconds; final full regression passed 180 files / 2,356 tests in 148.81 seconds, adding 31 cases. Exact evidence is recorded in `odd/evidence/agent-core-phase5/active-run-recovery.json`. Actual PostgreSQL passes 22 assertions inside a rolled-back local transaction. Claimed and final JSON snapshots returned by that real SQL function are then consumed by the compiled ESM reader under an ambient lease. This isolated reader compiles with strict declaration checking and no `skipLibCheck`; the broader legacy SDK WebAuthn declaration conflict described above remains unresolved. No live Supabase SDK/provider/WhatsApp calls or production schema changes occurred.

This unit contains the shared action/checkpoint contracts, read-only inspector/leased reader, prepared RPC/index, tests and evidence. No production composition or scheduler path changes. Inventory is 313 JS / 102 TS files, including twelve transitional declarations; no legacy JS implementation was retired. Rollback removes this disconnected reader and its prepared RPC/index while retaining the prior execution checkpoints and namespace fences. Full maintained-source TypeScript conversion, independent effect/delivery receipts, final active-run reconciliation/handoff, provider evaluation and rollout gates remain open.

## Phase 5 increment: receipt-based active-run closure and mixed-engine handoff

`reconcileActiveRun` can now close an interrupted owned execution and preserve its history in a typed mixed conversation. It consumes independently supplied domain receipts, a receipt covering delivery for the whole prior turn and an authenticated baseline conversation. It has no executor or sender port. Claimed and final source checkpoints remain intact, including unknown action fences; a separate `agentCoreActiveResolution` records the verified interpretation and the complete source snapshot.

Every executed/failed/fact-bearing action needs a receipt bound to the user, active run, call ID and SHA-256 of the exact persisted action. Applied outcomes require complete typed records, the matching operation and target, and explicit coverage of companion effects. A proven no-effect outcome imports no new records. Delivery must establish either accepted provider IDs or that nothing was sent. Existing record presence, final model text and success flags are insufficient proof. The injected audit/provider ports are trusted backend boundaries; actual production adapters proving these outcomes are still required before activation.

The host requires reconciliation and handoff capabilities, current lease identity, fresh ownership and cancellation/deadline checks across all evidence lookups and closure. A fifteen-second bound also applies to stalled ports. Pending prepared actions remain blocked; this increment does not cancel or abandon them. Entity/project/conversation operations are outside the mixed engine's supported task/reminder/calendar references and remain blocked. Partial results, unconfirmed companions, conflicting final resource snapshots, open transcripts and full reference/request ledgers require explicit handling rather than silently dropping continuity.

The prepared backend-only `resolve_hermes_active_run` RPC reuses the locked reader to compare the entire owned source: current anchor, older turn, original response, run context and current action payloads/receipts. It then retires the transient claim, closes the run and marks the prior inbound turn completed with its certificate in one transaction. Duplicate or stale closures return false; a closure does not reopen a run or modify the original prepared-action fences. SQL enforces ownership, structural receipt coverage and terminal transitions; host validation supplies the full typed semantic and canonical SHA checks. Raw service-role SQL access remains a trusted backend privilege, not a public evidence API.

Mixed-engine recovery accepts that certificate only after revalidating original source identity/fingerprint, every action proof, the terminal result and the exact conversation reconstructed from the baseline plus verified records. History and prior references stay preserved, and the resolved request joins the handled ledger. Invalid certificates retain the existing `PRIOR_TURN_RECONCILIATION_REQUIRED` error contract. Reminder-only readers/resolvers, active claim/read paths and inbound admission reject the new namespace. The handoff carries `grantsExecution: false`; later writes still require the current turn's normal host authorization.

Validation: strict project/package checks passed; 26 focused files / 511 tests passed in 5.58 seconds; final full regression passed 181 files / 2,385 tests in 40.48 seconds, adding 29 cases. Verification is recorded in `odd/evidence/agent-core-phase5/active-run-resolution.json`. Actual PostgreSQL passes 97 combined assertions: 33 claim, 19 reminder-only closure, 22 recovery reader and 23 active-run closure, all inside one rolled-back transaction. A strict emitted ESM fixture reads actual PostgreSQL state, builds a certificate using simulated trusted audit ports, closes it through actual SQL and verifies the resulting handoff with emitted TypeScript. Two actual PostgreSQL connections prove that one closure wins while a duplicate waits on the owner lock and is rejected. The scratch database is removed afterward. No live domain/provider/WhatsApp call, production migration or production advisor run occurred.

This unit includes evidence/resolution contracts, the bounded resolver/store/semantic handoff validator, mixed recovery integration, namespace fences, prepared closure SQL and tests. Inventory is 313 JS / 105 TS files with twelve transitional declarations and no legacy JS retirement. The isolated emitted recovery path remains strict without `skipLibCheck`; the broader legacy Supabase WebAuthn declaration issue remains a separate runtime-build gate. Rollback disables/removes this disconnected resolver and prepared SQL while retaining persisted certificates, source evidence and namespace fences wherever a closure has occurred. Production audit/sender/baseline adapters, handling of pending/unsupported active runs, router composition, provider evaluation, full maintained-source TypeScript conversion and rollout gates remain open.

## Phase 5 increment: persisted independently verified evidence

The reconciler can now consume effect, delivery and baseline receipts from a real PostgreSQL registry through `createActiveRunEvidenceRegistry`. Its strict envelope binds the verification authority, user, active/inbound run, thread, original response fingerprint and evidence key. Shared per-effect validation checks the supported domain, exact current action fingerprint, operation, target and companion coverage before publication and after reading.

The legacy audit does not supply this proof automatically. Existing execution events record transitions/results without independently verified operation correlation. Message writes are best effort; an absent message cannot prove that the sender was never called. Existing resource presence and legacy success flags remain insufficient. The new registry stores already verified receipts; live domain/provider verification producers and delivery instrumentation still need implementation before production activation.

Publication requires the separate `agent_core.evidence.register` host capability and current ambient lease. Reads require reconciliation access. Both capture private credentials, compare the entire owned recovery snapshot under SQL locks, bound uncooperative transports to fifteen seconds, recheck ownership and validate returned receipts. Missing, foreign or malformed rows stop recovery; they never become no-effect/no-send conclusions or an empty baseline. Baseline publication rejects open/system transcripts, duplicate identities and owner mismatches.

The prepared `hermes_verified_active_evidence` table enables RLS and grants the backend role only SELECT/INSERT. No frontend role can read or publish receipts, and the backend cannot update/delete them directly. Account/run/inbound cleanup can cascade through their foreign keys. A unique owner/run/source/kind/key index makes exact duplicate publication idempotent; a different receipt cannot overwrite the original. `access_hermes_active_evidence` is a private security-invoker RPC with an empty search path. If the lease expires after an insert boundary, the function raises to roll back that publication statement rather than committing under expired ownership. Direct service-role SQL access remains a trusted backend privilege; it does not prove factual verification on its own.

Actual SQL closure and emitted handoff validation preserve history and original evidence. Two PostgreSQL connections still demonstrate one winning closure and a rejected duplicate; concurrent receipt publication itself has not been separately tested. The temporary database is removed. No live domain/provider/WhatsApp call or production migration occurs.

## Phase 5 increment: transactional task completion/deletion receipts

`createAtomicTaskExecutor` supplies a real database outcome producer for completing or deleting one owned task identified by an exact UUID. It fits the existing owned bridge's injected executor boundary and requires its own host authorization and `agent_core.atomic_tasks` capability. Name/prefix targets, batches, extra parameters, other operations and fallback to the legacy executor are excluded. The active JS router remains unchanged.

The prepared `execute_hermes_atomic_task` RPC validates the live inbound lease, owned running claim and exactly one persisted unknown fence with the same canonical domain call, operation and target. It locks the task and linked reminders, then commits the task mutation, companion handling and immutable receipt together. An error while inserting the receipt rolls back both business changes. Completion acknowledges eligible owned reminders; deletion cancels pending linked reminders before the task FK detaches them. Processing/leased or foreign-owned companions, more than 100 linked reminders, invalid entity ownership and legacy rows outside typed record limits remain blocked before effects. Cancelled reminders are not resurrected.

The task receipt contains the exact operation, normalized result, legacy presentation result and companion IDs. A duplicate execution under the same valid fence returns the same receipt without changing the task again. A response lost after transaction commit remains uncertain to the caller; no automatic retry occurs. `createAtomicTaskEffectSource` later reads the immutable journal under a fresh owned recovery snapshot, checks user/run/thread/call/target and the canonical domain call, and derives an applied proof through the shared verifier. This proof comes from the task transaction itself. A missing journal entry cannot prove no effect. The original unknown action fence remains available for audit.

A strict emitted executor performed one actual local task deletion with companion cancellation and receipt creation; its transport deliberately lost the response after commit. The emitted recovery source then read that transaction's receipt, and reconciliation/actual SQL closure/emitted handoff succeeded without another task mutation. Two real connections exercised one winning closure and a rejected waiting duplicate. The temporary database was removed. Evidence: `odd/evidence/agent-core-phase5/atomic-task-receipts.json`.

The domain proof is real in this fixture; delivery and baseline evidence producers remain simulated. There is no live provider/WhatsApp call, Supabase SDK service-role round trip, production advisor/migration run or router activation. Concurrent first task mutation is not separately certified by this fixture; duplicate retrieval and closure concurrency are covered. The prepared tables/RPCs use private backend permissions, RLS and an empty search path. Isolated emitted compilation remains strict without `skipLibCheck`; the broader legacy SDK declaration gate remains open.

## Phase 5 increment: durable delivery manifests and provider acceptance receipts

`createDeliveryManifest` seals the entire ordered reply plan against the owned final active-run checkpoint before dispatch. The prepared private RPC atomically stores its immutable source/plan and appends an `agentCoreDeliveryManifest` marker to the inbound response. Up to twelve uniquely identified messages are supported. The old admission/reminder/mixed-state paths reject this new namespace rather than treating an unfamiliar checkpoint as a fresh turn. The active-run recovery reader accepts the marker so its full source CAS can bind delivery evidence.

Each send requires the private live inbound lease, `agent_core.delivery`, explicit host authorization and supported-content validation. SQL persists `ready → attempted` before the transport is invoked; only accepted earlier messages allow the next attempt. The sender then persists the exact provider message identity as `accepted`. Transport uncertainty or failed receipt persistence retains `unknown` or `attempted`; neither permits replay. A stale local ready plan cannot bypass the database fence. If acceptance commits but its RPC response is lost, the attempted downgrade is rejected and the accepted receipt survives. RPC and transport stages are bounded to fifteen seconds and ownership is checked again around storage/dispatch.

`createKapsoTextTransport` captures an explicit backend user/recipient/channel binding and sends the existing Kapso text request shape once. It accepts exactly one nonempty provider message identity. This first adapter supports text only, with a conservative host limit of 4,000 characters; CTA/flow delivery needs its own implementation and fails validation before its send fence. It does not write the old single-message checkpoint. No provider retry, implicit recipient lookup or fallback is introduced.

The recovery producer reads the manifest against the entire freshly owned snapshot, validates the original source plus marker and returns whole-turn acceptance evidence only when every planned message is accepted. A sealed plan whose entries are all ready can prove no attempt under this exclusively instrumented send path; a missing ledger, uncertain attempt or partially accepted plan cannot prove whole-turn delivery. These guarantees require the future runtime composition to route every send through the manifest. They do not certify historical legacy turns or independent sends outside this path. Provider acceptance is not handset delivery.

Validation: strict project/package checks passed; 29 focused files / 559 tests passed in 6.51 seconds; the full suite passed 184 files / 2,433 tests in 41.65 seconds, adding twelve cases. Local PostgreSQL passed 183 combined assertions: the previous 162 plus 21 manifest checks, inside a rolled-back transaction. Those checks cover owner/source fences, ordered attempts, acceptance identities, non-replay, downgrade rejection and private access. A strict emitted ESM fixture performs an actual local task mutation and receipt, seals two replies, verifies the attempted state before each simulated HTTP request, commits both acceptance receipts and deliberately loses the second acceptance RPC response. Recovery reads the real task journal and manifest, reconciles through actual SQL closure and validates the emitted handoff without repeating either send or task mutation. Two PostgreSQL connections demonstrate one winning closure and a rejected waiting duplicate. The temporary database is removed. Evidence: `odd/evidence/agent-core-phase5/delivery-manifest.json`.

The fixture's domain and delivery persistence/producers are real; HTTP acceptance responses and the owned conversation baseline producer are simulated. Seven actual manifest mutation RPCs and one recovery read run in the fixture. No live provider/WhatsApp call, production database/migration operation, Supabase SDK service-role round trip or router activation occurs. Concurrent first dispatch itself is not separately exercised; closure concurrency is. The mutable monotonic manifest table uses RLS and backend SELECT/INSERT/UPDATE privileges; direct service-role SQL remains a trusted backend boundary. Isolated emitted compilation remains strict without `skipLibCheck`.

Inventory is 313 JS / 115 TS files, including twelve transitional declarations; no maintained JS implementation was retired. The model-neutral gateway and the existing scheduler are preserved.


## Phase 5 increment: authoritative owned baseline and typed recovery composition

`createOwnedBaseline` captures a conversation baseline before an owned suspended active run is claimed. The private SQL RPC returns the exact latest completed queue row for the leased thread, or an explicit genesis candidate only when there are no older queue rows. Any older non-completed state blocks capture, including uncertain and queued turns; it cannot be skipped to manufacture empty history. The candidate binds the user, active/inbound identities, request, thread and sequence. The host reconstructs its conversation only from a strict completed mixed checkpoint or a semantically verified active-run handoff. Legacy/reminder-only, foreign, open, malformed or partially delivered checkpoints require a separate migration/handoff and are rejected.

Capture requires `agent_core.baseline.capture` and current private lease ownership. It compares the complete candidate again in SQL and atomically inserts an immutable receipt plus `agentCoreBaseline` marker. It preserves closed history, domain references and handled-request identities, rejects duplicates/current-request replay and never compacts or fills missing history. The backend has SELECT/INSERT privileges on the RLS-protected ledger, with no direct UPDATE/DELETE; account/inbound cleanup may cascade. The RPC is a private security invoker with an empty search path, following the [Supabase function security guidance](https://supabase.com/docs/guides/database/functions). Direct service-role SQL remains trusted; structural SQL checks do not replace host semantic validation.

Recovery reads that receipt against the full owned active-run snapshot, binds its marker/run/turn/thread/sequence, reconstructs the conversation again from the recorded candidate and rejects altered stored conversation data. Missing receipts remain unavailable. All RPC stages are bounded to fifteen seconds, including uncooperative transports, with cancellation and ownership checked before and after. A response lost during capture remains storage-uncertain without automatic retry; a baseline-only interrupted turn is not automatically admitted for effects and still needs a separate recovery path.

`createActiveRunRecoveryRuntime` now composes the actual SQL recovery reader, transactional task outcome source, whole-turn delivery manifest, owned baseline producer and resolution store. It performs no model calls, proof publication through the generic registry, domain replay or delivery replay. It returns null only for the reader's explicit absence result; otherwise it reconciles and closes using those independently persisted sources. This is the recovery portion of runtime composition; foreground inference/approval execution, presentation/delivery orchestration and the production router remain separate and disconnected. Task proof coverage remains limited to exact-UUID completion/deletion, and the manifest transport remains text-only.

The new marker is excluded from reminder/mixed admission and the prepared reminder-only SQL resolver. Active-run execution can admit it once because its claim preserves the captured baseline; it still rejects existing active/delivery checkpoints and repeated lease execution. Prior active-run recovery accepts the baseline namespace so full source CAS and evidence validation can use it. The marker alone never grants execution or certifies legacy delivery.

Five real recovery RPCs read all three proof sources and close in SQL; emitted handoff verification preserves history and original evidence. A stale duplicate closure is rejected and the temporary database is removed. No generic verified-evidence rows are supplied in this fixture. The checkpoint data is fixture-seeded and provider HTTP responses are simulated; this is not a live production/history/provider certification. First-capture concurrency and the whole foreground runtime have not been separately exercised.

## Phase 5 increment: owned approval execution and deterministic delivery composition

`createActiveRunTurnRuntime` connects baseline capture, the owned execution bridge, exact-UUID atomic task mutations, deterministic WhatsApp presentation and sealed delivery into one isolated backend composition. It accepts an already planned, snapshot-bound approval decision through the model-neutral contract; inference itself remains in the portable decision wrapper. This unit covers a generic suspended run whose actions are all prepared tasks with no prior execution facts, selected completion/deletion targets already bound to UUIDs, and cancellation. Continuations, other domains, name/batch targets, in-flight claims, malformed call identities, profile updates and unsupported states are rejected before baseline persistence. Refusal and unrelated/supersede decisions perform no writes or sends.

The runtime captures private owner credentials, detached snapshot/decision/user data and host capabilities. It verifies owner/thread/request and proposal fingerprint, captures the owned baseline, then uses the actual `ActiveRunExecutionBridge` with the actual SQL store and transactional task executor. The host's write authorization still runs immediately before each persisted dispatch fence. An optional private receipt observer on the atomic executor exposes a detached copy only after its normal owner/domain/call validation; the composition additionally binds every receipt to the acquired claim, selected action and target. Neither the observer nor the model supplies presentation facts. Domain mutation errors, unconfirmed outcomes or incomplete receipt coverage stop before success presentation.

The existing `WhatsAppProductivityPresenter` renders the transaction receipts with the current card vocabulary and deduplication. Model `reply` prose is ignored; cancellation uses a deterministic bilingual acknowledgment. The composition reconstructs the final response from acknowledged claim/action/finish writes, including the baseline marker, then seals it against the entire actual SQL response before dispatch. A mismatch cannot send. Supported text is delivered once through the manifest's write-before-send fence with separate host delivery authorization. CTAs/flows and text over the transport's 4,000-character limit are unsupported here; presentation failures after a confirmed mutation retain durable source/operation evidence for reconciliation and do not invent a successful delivery.

One constructed runtime admits one attempt, including concurrent calls: admission is checked again after the first asynchronous lease assertion. Storage/dispatch failures do not reopen it. Reconstructing another instance cannot bypass the stored baseline/claim/delivery fences. A turn deadline is capped at sixty seconds and active-run expiry; combined cancellation bounds the outer operation even when a dependency ignores its signal. Underlying requests may already have committed, so cancellation never proves no effect. The existing bridge/store and SQL lease/claim boundaries still determine whether a late operation can commit.

The last stops with reconciliation required and makes zero send attempts. Across all four fixtures there are three local task mutations and three simulated HTTP requests, with no replay. The first three recover through the actual typed recovery composition, SQL closure and handoff while preserving prior history. ## Phase 5 increment: atomic inbound completion and accepted exchange history

The isolated approval composition now returns `completed` only after the accepted reply and resulting conversation are durably published with inbound completion. Baseline capture accepts an optional strict input record containing the authenticated host's current user text, language and timezone. New composed turns require that record before claim/mutation; its fingerprint and `inputRecorded` flag are preserved in the baseline marker. Existing baseline receipts without input remain readable through their preceding paths, but cannot be silently upgraded into a completed exchange. User text is supplied by the authenticated host, never by the model's approval `reply`.

`read_hermes_active_turn_completion` supplies the current owner's actual baseline, complete accepted manifest, task journal receipts and full response under a live lease. `buildActiveTurnCompletion` validates those sources, binds marker/owner/run/request/thread/sequence and claim/action/target/operation, checks complete ordered receipt coverage, and re-renders the exact accepted response. It preserves prior history and references, merges transaction-linked task records, appends the captured user message and accepted assistant message once, and extends the handled-request ledger. Input, response, record and handled-history tampering fail semantic validation. History/reference capacity is checked before business mutation; no silent truncation or fabricated empty history is introduced. Linked reminder references retain their baseline historical fields; this unit does not synthesize current acknowledgment timestamps from companion IDs.

The completion certificate uses a compact baseline projection rather than embedding its predecessor's entire response. Its lineage binds the immutable baseline ID, candidate/input fingerprints and original source. Semantic revalidation reconstructs the expected conversation and card from the recorded facts; the SQL publisher additionally compares the actual immutable baseline and task journal plus the mutable monotonic accepted manifest. Persisted completions are trusted backend records, not standalone signed certificates or evidence of handset delivery. Direct service-role SQL remains a trusted boundary, and host semantic validation remains necessary beside structural SQL gates.

`finish_hermes_active_turn` locks the owned queue row, run, actions and manifest; compares the entire expected response, actual run/context/expiry/waiting state and current action receipts; and requires the exact stored baseline, journal and accepted delivery inputs. It checks history-prefix preservation and exact captured-user/accepted-assistant messages. Publication appends `agentCoreActiveCompletion`, marks the inbound row completed, clears its request/error and retires its lease/token atomically. If ownership expires after the update, a serialization error rolls back history, status, cleanup and retirement together. A stale or duplicate finalizer cannot reopen or overwrite a completed row. The private security-invoker functions use an empty search path and no frontend execute grants.

The finalizer has its own `agent_core.turn.finalize` capability and bounded, cancellation-aware reads/writes. It checks ownership before closing and deliberately does not renew a successfully retired lease. A lost finalization response remains storage-uncertain to its caller, while a committed certificate can be imported through `recoverActiveTurnCompletion`, the mixed state-recovery port and the next owned baseline capture. Both prior and current exchanges are preserved. If a send's acceptance RPC response is lost, the composition reads the actual accepted manifest before finalizing; it never sends again or infers acceptance from caller memory. Missing/attempted/unknown ledger state remains unavailable.

New input-recorded interrupted turns are excluded from the older generic active-run reconciler, which preserved only the baseline history. They need their dedicated completion/interruption path and cannot be closed by that older path while dropping the current exchange. A lost task response before a manifest exists still stops without a success send and remains unresolved. Baseline-only interruption, failed acquisition and unknown inbound recovery under a later owner's lease remain pending. Completed turns do not require effect/delivery replay.

Actual local PostgreSQL passed 225 combined assertions: the previous 205 plus twenty completion checks, all rolled back. The preceding prepared baseline/admission/resolution functions are rebuilt with their current input/namespace rules before regression fixtures execute. New checks cover captured input, unsent/attempted blocking, exact persisted sources, foreign/stale ownership, forged input/history/delivery rejection, atomic completion/cleanup/retirement, private permissions and a delayed expired write that must roll back every closure change.

Five disposable PostgreSQL scenarios run the actual strict-checked TypeScript source through `tsx` and the legacy execution bridge: normal completion, cancellation without task dispatch, lost committed acceptance response, lost committed finalization response, and lost committed task response. The first three close normally; the fourth reports uncertainty but its completed row restores the full exchange; the fifth makes no send and remains unresolved. Across the fixtures there are four local task mutations and four simulated provider HTTP requests, with no replay. All four completed cases feed their preserved current/prior history into the next real owned baseline capture. Temporary databases are removed and live HTTP is forbidden in the harness. Initial history and HTTP responses are fixture data; no production history/provider/Supabase SDK service-role round trip is certified. Concurrent first finalization is not separately exercised; retired duplicate denial and rollback are covered.

## Phase 5 increment: strict TypeScript worker and lease publication protocol

The maintained inbound worker and ambient lease implementations are now strict TypeScript in `src/jobs/inbound-turn-worker.ts` and `src/agent/core/turn-lease.ts`. Their old JS paths contain only compatibility wiring to compiled output. The handwritten lease declaration is removed, and the existing worker regression test is converted to TypeScript. The maintained JS inventory decreases by one physical test file; two executable implementations have moved to TS while their two JS import shims remain for existing callers. Full source conversion still includes eventually retiring those shims.

The typed worker keeps the current legacy request handler, queue serialization/concurrency, polling and heartbeat defaults. It adds a separate `processOwnedTurn` backend callback that receives the private claimed owner independently from message JSON; the legacy `processTurn` callback keeps its one-argument behavior. The owned credentials are frozen and never added to model context or response history. Ports explicitly type claim/renew/checkpoint/finish/resolution and optional completion observation. Storage operations and publication are bounded to fifteen seconds, including uncooperative promises; handlers themselves remain responsible for their own inference/execution deadlines.

`finalizeCurrent` admits one publication synchronously, stops heartbeats, drains any renewal already in flight and verifies ownership again before calling the finalizer. The completion adapter uses this optional boundary when running inside the worker. Acknowledged publication retires the local lease and skips the old post-handler renewal/finish path. Later effects cannot assert that owner. If publication throws or remains unconfirmed, the worker does not renew, perform a legacy downgrade or replay the handler. A known completed turn remains completed even if its handler subsequently throws. Ordinary legacy handler failures still use the existing owned unknown finish path.

The prepared additive migration stores the publishing token as a private `completion_lease_token` receipt key at the same atomic completion boundary that clears the active lease. `was_completed_hermes_active_turn` confirms a completed certificate only for that exact inbound identity/retired nonce, with matching request/thread/sequence. The receipt key never authorizes renewals or active effects. The observer is a private security-invoker RPC with an empty search path, consistent with the [Supabase function security guidance](https://supabase.com/docs/guides/database/functions). Existing table permissions/RLS keep the receipt key inaccessible to frontend roles; direct service-role SQL remains trusted. The default backend adapter exposes this observer, but ordinary legacy turns do not call it or require this prepared migration until typed publication is admitted.

A publication response lost after commit is handled by observing that receipt rather than calling finish again. An unavailable/unconfirmed observer leaves uncertainty intact and lets the existing queue expiry policy prevent replay. It does not certify no effect/no delivery or repair an interrupted new-format turn. Baseline-only/unknown new-format reconciliation remains pending.

Mixed source/emitted loaders exposed a real integration failure: they could instantiate two ambient contexts and make a compiled worker appear unleased to a source TS adapter. The TS implementation now shares one versioned, non-writable/non-configurable `AsyncLocalStorage` cell per process and rejects an incompatible existing cell. Source and compiled callers retain the same scoped owner, while background `withoutTurnLease` isolation remains unchanged. This is backend runtime coordination; it adds no model authority.

`tsconfig.runtime.json` emits only the pure worker/lease runtime into ignored `dist/runtime`, without importing the legacy SDK implementation into the typed build. Package build/typecheck/test entry points emit that runtime; `start` remains a direct Node command for compiler-free production images. Build once before starting from a clean checkout (`pnpm run build`); development/evaluation entry points build first. During TS worker development, run `pnpm exec tsc -p tsconfig.runtime.json --watch` beside the current Node watcher, or rebuild before testing source changes. This unit does not introduce a combined development watcher.

The Docker build now includes workspace manifests/source for strict compilation and copies the emitted runtime into a production-only dependency image. It excludes nested node_modules and incremental build metadata. A clean container build caught cached TS build information whose emitted files had been excluded; correcting those exclusions restores a genuine cold build. Production boot uses JavaScript output and needs neither `tsx` nor a compiler/TS loader. The CI import check also loads the emitted worker. Full emitted application migration and the broader legacy SDK declaration issue remain separate gates.

Actual PostgreSQL passed 235 combined assertions: the preceding 225 plus ten retired-receipt/identity/permission checks, inside one rolled-back transaction. The new receipt migration is applied before the preceding completion fixtures so their expired-write rollback and publication checks execute with the new finalizer body.

Normal completion, cancellation and lost acceptance response produce no legacy finish or renewal after close. A lost finalization response performs exactly one owned completion observation with no downgrade; its history still becomes the next actual baseline. ## Phase 5 increment: migrate maintained active-run execution to strict TypeScript

Approval and continuation now have one maintained implementation in `src/agent/core/active-run-executor.ts`. `agent-run.js` supplies the existing database, schedule resolver, execution-fact presenter and working-memory ports to the compiled factory; existing imports continue to call the same API. It no longer contains the two execution bodies. Run creation, cancellation of superseded drafts and prompt formatting still live in that JS lifecycle module and remain explicit migration work. The compatibility declaration derives its signatures and exports its types from the real TS implementation instead of maintaining separate handwritten executor contracts.

The pure executor types prepared actions, runs, domain calls, execution context, observations and storage boundaries. Invalid domain calls may omit tool names/parameters; incomplete or invalid continuation stays pending or fails without dispatch. JSON schedule inspection narrows values before accessing nested fields. Result types preserve the legacy optional/undefined first-result fields rather than falsely promising that every in-memory result is already JSON. There is no runtime provider/SDK import, `any`, `@ts-ignore` or relaxed compiler option in this implementation. Provider-specific code stays behind the tool port.

Durable write fences, authorization-before-write, selected-action bookkeeping, no replay of already executed actions, failure/unknown short-circuiting, original schedule anchors and deterministic fact/working-memory updates retain their existing behavior. A lost claim now observes actions through the injected runtime instead of bypassing it to use global legacy storage. This closes a host-isolation gap; both approval and continuation have regression coverage for it. Full input/decision validation remains owned by the existing host adapters, not by this extraction.

The selective runtime build emits the executor alongside the worker/lease. CI imports the emitted executor. Docker now copies all source into its build stage so new configured runtime roots are present; it still compiles only the selected roots and ships compiled JS with production dependencies. An initial image smoke caught the previous two-file COPY list silently excluding the new root; rebuilding with the corrected source copy passes. Starting from a clean checkout still requires `pnpm run build`; the production image uses Node directly without a TypeScript compiler or loader.

## Phase 5 increment: strict TypeScript suspended-run lifecycle and prompt

All maintained run-lifecycle logic has moved from `agent-run.js` into `active-run-lifecycle.ts`: active lookup, creation/staging, exact supersession, activation, pending cancellation and default execution acquisition. `active-run-prompt.ts` owns the pure prompt renderer. Together with the preceding typed executor, the original module is now 49 lines of host dependency wiring rather than 361 lines containing lifecycle/prompt logic. Existing JS call paths still work through emitted code. The compatibility declaration derives every public signature from the maintained TS factories and exports their actual types; the shim and declaration remain until callers/build conversion permits their retirement.

The lifecycle preserves the storage protocol: running requests are not replaced; candidates are initially inert (`failed`); durable staging precedes retirement/activation; failed retirement never activates a candidate; uncertain activation retains staged data and makes the existing conditional demotion attempt. Existing options for exact supersession, preserving an active run, reference time/timezone and default fifteen-minute TTL retain their behavior. Ports type creation options, staged actions, owner-scoped lookups and conditional status changes. Storage methods are resolved at invocation time, preserving live host bindings and existing mocks. This extraction adds no per-call deadline; the existing host remains responsible for bounding storage/handler operations.

One cleanup edge case is fixed: a secondary cleanup failure can no longer mask the original staging failure when the thrown object is frozen or primitive. Mutable errors retain optional `cleanupError` metadata. Supported prompt payloads retain complete draft fields, exact call IDs, temporal anchors and strict-wire field guidance. Two pre-migration fixture outputs check approval/continuation prompt equality byte for byte; narrowing JSON fields also avoids assuming arbitrary stored values are arrays.

The offline Groq comparison no longer extracts a function body from the retired JS implementation. It reads only the allowlisted pure TS presenter, strips types using Node's built-in `stripTypeScriptTypes`, and evaluates it through the existing deny-by-default module adapter. The original TS source and legacy wiring are recorded in provenance/policy fingerprints. Lifecycle/execution/database modules are never evaluated by this adapter. Two new checks verify original-source hashing (including erased type-only changes) and rejection of an added effect-owning dependency. Initial broad regression caught the old source-extraction dependency; the corrected complete suite passes without relaxing the evaluator's import controls or changing the canonical schema identities.

## Phase 5 increment: inspect interrupted turns with captured input and actual receipts

`interrupted-active-completion.ts` adds a strict, effects-free reader and inspector for the recorded-input completion namespace. It reconstructs a verifiable completion candidate only from an owned baseline, the current persisted run/action snapshot, independent atomic task receipts and an accepted delivery manifest. Captured user text and the accepted deterministic reply are appended exactly once to the retained baseline history. The report always sets `grantsExecution: false` and `allowsHandoff: false`: even a verifiable candidate is not published, does not complete the queue row and cannot be imported as history while that row remains unknown. Atomic publication under the later owner's lease is the next separate unit.

The new prepared backend-only invoker RPC, `read_hermes_interrupted_active_completion`, binds nonce/request/thread and requires a live current lease. It selects the oldest unknown predecessor through the existing locked recovery reader, then supplies immutable baseline/task receipts and the locked delivery manifest. The current owner is rechecked before returning. Evidence is bounded to thirteen receipts/actions so a thirteenth fails the contracts instead of silently hiding an oversized set. An unavailable owner/foreign source returns SQL NULL; explicit `{ absent: true }` means no interrupted predecessor, never permission to initialize empty history. Missing ledgers remain nullable evidence and are not inferred from resource existence. The function performs no publication, business mutation, delivery, lease retirement or cleanup.

The shared source validator retains the original legacy guard against recorded-input history loss. A dedicated recorded-input inspection path additionally requires an unknown prior turn and an unchanged pending-state payload. Both paths enforce source/owner ordering, actual action identity/status/facts, exact context and microsecond expiry equivalence. New contract exports describe the complete evidence envelope. Incomplete execution, missing baseline/manifest, unconfirmed delivery or an unverifiable completion are reported without a certificate; changed authoritative owner/run/baseline/source data is rejected. Supported certification remains scoped to completed/cancelled atomic-task approvals with accepted text delivery. Baseline-only interruptions, a lost execution response, unsupported domains and delivery uncertainty remain blocked. Neither the existing recovery loader nor the production router is changed to automatically accept this report.

## Phase 5 increment: transactional publication of interrupted completions

`interrupted-completion-recovery-runtime.ts` closes one verifiable historical turn under the later owner's live lease. It reads actual persisted evidence, reconstructs the strict certificate, submits one publication and validates the returned durable row through the existing completion importer. The current turn remains running, its response is untouched, and neither a dispatch nor sender port exists. The result sets `requiresHistoryReload: true` and `grantsExecution: false`: closing the oldest unknown is not permission to skip another unresolved turn or treat that historical conversation as the current baseline.

Two prepared private invoker RPCs publish and observe the exact historical certificate. Publication rereads the full evidence envelope under owned locks and requires full snapshot/baseline/manifest/receipt equality. It repeats persisted run/action identity/final-state checks and requires accepted text delivery plus the actual immutable task receipt set. SQL reconstructs the full conversation: all previous history, reminders/events and handled IDs remain intact, and only exact receipt task records are merged in stable reference order. Captured user input and accepted reply are appended once. Certificate/status/request cleanup/old lease clearing commit together, with a post-write expiry check that raises to roll back a slow trigger or write. The current nonce never becomes a receipt key on the historical row, and the current lease is not retired. Existing active-owner finalization is unchanged.

If publication throws or returns a transport/storage error, the runtime observes once through the later owner's live lease. Observation requires exact prior source and certificate identity/content; it never performs another write or infers a commit from missing evidence. Deterministic conflicts are not retried. Cancellation or ownership loss blocks observation/admission. Constructed runtimes admit only one invocation, including concurrent calls. Recovery requires separate reconciliation/handoff capabilities and must precede current-turn checkpointing; SQL enforces the same current namespace boundary. The overall operation has a thirty-second signal cap and each publication/observation RPC a fifteen-second cap; the reader retains its own bound. Full semantic/fingerprint validation belongs to the trusted TS backend, while SQL compares actual source/ledgers and exact conversation content. Service-role direct SQL remains trusted. No additional table or public endpoint is introduced; execution grants and empty search paths follow the [Supabase function security guidance](https://supabase.com/docs/guides/database/functions).

## Phase 5 increment: owned recovery before new-runtime planning

`createOwnedProductivityAssistant` wires the actual `DurableProductivityAssistant` to an owned recovery port. The runner and sender remain injected through model-independent interfaces. Recovery precedes the current checkpoint, model, domain tools and delivery. Caller-provided history overrides are rejected. This factory is isolated from the production router/worker handler; it does not activate V2 or change the scheduler.

The prepared private invoker RPC `read_hermes_productivity_turn_entry` authenticates the current nonce/request/thread, requires a live lease and rejects already-started managed namespaces. Every older queued/running row blocks admission. Otherwise it returns the oldest unknown predecessor or latest completed checkpoint, with a minimal current anchor. Explicit `prior: null` is authenticated genesis; SQL NULL/error/timeout never initializes an empty conversation. Queries use projected columns, existing status-specific indexes and one ordered predecessor. No request body or private lease credential enters the returned history. The reader performs no write and checks current ownership again before returning.

Completed mixed checkpoints, active completions and verified legacy handoffs use the extracted shared semantic importer, preserving its reconciliation error contract. An unknown recorded-input active turn may invoke the existing independently verified closure once. Its fresh evidence must match the exact originally admitted prior row before publication. Lost committed replies are observed once without another write. A second authoritative entry read then selects history across all predecessors; another unknown row still blocks all current work. If a later completed checkpoint exists, its conversation is imported rather than replacing it with the just-closed older history. Unknown legacy/mixed/reminder namespaces, missing receipts, unsupported domains, unfinished execution and unconfirmed delivery remain blocked. Admission is single-use, snapshots the private owner/capabilities, observes cancellation/lease loss, caps entry reads at fifteen seconds and the recovery operation at sixty seconds.

## Phase 5 increment: strict TypeScript persistence for runs and inbound turns

The existing agent-run and inbound queue modules now delegate their implementation to emitted TypeScript. `createAgentRunPersistence` owns run creation/lookup/expiry, conditional owner/status transitions, prepared-action staging/validation and execution events. `createInboundTurnPersistence` owns queue insertion, claim/renew/checkpoint/reconciliation/observation/finalization and readiness checks. Both accept narrow service-side client ports. Legacy callers retain their existing exports through small JS wiring modules, with declaration signatures derived directly from the factories.

Client access is lazy because `database/index.js` re-exports the run module before initializing Supabase. This avoids introducing a module-initialization cycle. The host still owns configuration and its shared ambient lease-aware transport. Configured database failures remain errors and cannot load cached/offline state as success. Each constructed run store has separate offline state, while the legacy facade retains one shared instance. Kind normalization, staged activation, exact owner/status/expiry predicates, prepared-row checks and existing query/cache/reference behavior are preserved. The queue validates its untyped RPC results: claims contain at most one identified turn with JSON request/response, insert results contain at most one ID, and acknowledgements must be actual booleans. Malformed/null acknowledgements fail with `INBOUND_STORAGE_UNAVAILABLE`; false remains a denied operation. Missing claim responses normalize to null. Ordinary completed rows are not converted into independently certified active completions.

The runtime compiler includes both persistence implementations. Their executable imports use production dependencies only; `@templelabs/contracts` and lifecycle/worker types are erased. Local JSON schemas use the installed production Zod package so the production runner does not need development workspace links. Queue operations retain the fifteen-second SDK signal and the existing transport/worker bounds; no application-level retries are added. [Supabase's current abort-signal documentation](https://supabase.com/docs/reference/javascript/using-modifiers-abortsignal), the installed SDK source and changelog were checked. No SDK version, client configuration, database schema or scheduler change occurs.

## Phase 5 increment: typed task/message persistence and observation-bound orchestration

Task and message persistence now live in emitted TypeScript factories, `createTaskPersistence` and `createMessagePersistence`. The original modules supply the shared client lazily and retain every public export/alias, with declarations derived from the factories. A narrow domain query port describes the consumed task/container/message projections without importing the Supabase SDK into the implementation. Deterministic entity selection, identifier normalization and the existing update lease fence remain host dependencies. Creation/deduplication/batch identity, owner predicates, named container refusal, priorities, date anchors, result/error envelopes, cascades and existing query behavior are preserved. Message persistence retains chronological bounded history, best-effort duplicate/error behavior, originating transport lookup and assistant-only ID correlation. This chat history remains separate from authoritative inbound/completion evidence.

`createObservedTargetResolver` moves one orchestration call boundary to TypeScript. Its injected completion port has no provider/model name or execution port. The legacy module still supplies the existing gateway by default, and an explicit completion can be used independently. An ambiguous result may only select an integer position among two to twelve observed candidates under the existing evidence-size cap. The model receives the compact candidate description rather than database IDs, and its response cannot change the requested action or other fields. Captured params and observations are copied before awaiting the completion, so caller/provider mutation cannot change the authorized operation or selected identity afterward. Invalid candidate objects/non-string IDs and fabricated response fields are rejected. Unknown, partial or successful operations retain the existing no-repair guard; execution still performs its own owner checks.

The runtime compiler includes both domain factories and the resolver; their workspace contract imports are type-only. Compatibility imports execute the emitted ESM on Node with production dependencies. No new schema, SDK dependency/configuration, provider selection, V2 route, scheduler or deployment is introduced. Legacy task completion/deletion and their reminder cascades retain their prior semantics; they are not converted into a new atomic receipt/certificate path by this migration. The separately implemented atomic-task producer and evidence-based recovery remain unchanged.

## Phase 5 increment: typed projects and entity creation/read/resolution

All project persistence and selection logic now lives in `createProjectPersistence`, with its original JS module reduced to client wiring and factory-derived declarations. Entity normalization, edit distance, matching, ambiguity/deletion selection, reference inspection, linked-task partitioning and metadata merging move to the pure `entity-resolution.ts`. `createEntityPersistence` owns creation/refinement, owner-scoped reference preflight, listing/mirror deduplication, pending task counts and ID lookup. The entity module retains destructive deletion/rename/merge/deduplication workflows in JS for the next separate migration, using the emitted helpers and the new creation/read port. No blanket declaration hides those remaining JS implementations.

Typechecking exposed an existing project metadata defect: the matched-project branch referenced `merged` without defining it and consequently returned null from its catch. It now computes `mergeMetadata(bestMatch.metadata,metadata)` before persisting the result, retaining previous non-empty/nested fields. Project metadata update, rename and legacy mirror rename additionally bind `user_id` alongside the selected ID. Existing UUID preservation, category refinement, matching thresholds, aliases, mirror/list behavior and best-effort errors remain compatible. Unavailable owned entity preflight still returns an error rather than an empty successful snapshot. The shared query boundary adds upsert and the consumed container/reminder metadata fields; it introduces no SDK import into the implementation.

Project deletion retains its owned sequential cascade and actual partial receipts. Dry runs and foreign-owner targets write nothing. Unfinished tasks are removed, completed task history remains, and each dependent write must return a receipt before the next one proceeds. A failed project write after reminder/task changes leaves those confirmed partial results visible and does not delete its entity mirror. This migration does not turn legacy cascades, fuzzy creation or best-effort mirror synchronization into an independent atomic effect certificate. Existing private producers, journal/manifest/completion paths, SQL, scheduler and provider/V2 routing are unchanged.

## Phase 5 increment: typed destructive entity workflows and uncertain outcomes

`createEntityMutationPersistence` now owns entity rename, merge, deletion and maintenance deduplication in strict TypeScript. The entity JS module is eight lines of wiring/re-exports; all of its declarations derive from the maintained resolution/read/write implementations. The compiler includes the new mutation root and the narrow query port adds consumed memory projections plus an optional legacy RPC interface. Existing object/positional signatures, aliases, owner predicates, matching behavior, metadata and ordinary result formats remain compatible. The legacy entity tool still has a small result-normalization change and a transitional `Promise<unknown>` declaration; its remaining dispatcher is a later TS unit.

The old rename/merge paths could apply an atomic RPC and then replay its work through compatibility code when the reply was lost. A single classified attempt now admits compatibility only for an absent host RPC or an explicit PGRST202/42883 missing-function result. Other storage errors, thrown/lost replies, unavailable data and malformed success envelopes return `ENTITY_MUTATION_RECONCILIATION_REQUIRED` with an unknown outcome. Rename requires the returned identity and non-negative integer cascade counts; merge requires exact source/target IDs, a task count and JSON metadata. No write retry or synthetic receipt occurs. Atomic success describes the existing SQL RPC, not the entire later mirror-maintenance sequence.

The compatibility merge previously ignored task-move errors and could continue deleting the source. It now requires observed task-move data, a returned target update and an exact source-deletion receipt before continuing. Rename propagates an uncertain nested merge, and maintenance stops at its first failed/unknown merge. Legacy rename text cascades now retain the owner predicate on reminder/task/memory updates. Entity deletion requires an exact observed entity set before dependent cleanup; absent/error/partial deletion results stop with the confirmed entity subset retained. Cleanup filters recheck pending/processing reminder state and pending/in-progress task state, so a task completed after the snapshot is preserved. Missing or incomplete cleanup results remain partial reconciliation rather than complete success. Ordering and best-effort project mirror behavior remain legacy and non-atomic.

The entity tool now preserves unknown rename/merge/deletion and interrupted deduplication outcomes, with localized operational messages and their data retained. It does not map uncertainty to entity-not-found or a successful maintenance response. Other best-effort legacy cascades remain outside independent receipts; this work does not certify generic entity effects for the isolated active-run recovery engine. No schema, RPC definition, SDK configuration/dependency, scheduler, V2 activation or deployment changes.

Existing canonical entity, matching/list, lifecycle/destructive-run and typed container tests pass through the original entry modules.

## Phase 5 increment: typed embedding services and preference compiler

## Phase 5 increment: typed semantic extraction, admission and retrieval service

Invalid/unavailable replies fail closed or throw when the caller requests strict admission. ## Phase 5 increment: typed structured temporal resolution and pending merges

`createScheduleResolver` now owns complete structured temporal resolution and pending schedule merge in strict TypeScript. The original typed-schedule JS entry only wires existing local date parts/timezone-to-UTC primitives lazily, with factory-derived declarations for both public functions. Existing behavior stays compatible: implicit minutes default to zero, structurally supplied day offsets can normalize local/calendar kinds, bounded relative minutes/days/weeks retain their limits, and explicit AM/PM/midnight/noon conversion is unchanged. Ambiguous 12-hour clocks select the nearest future candidate on the specified local date or retain the later same-date clock if both elapsed. Calendar date validity and timezone round-trips reject impossible dates and nonexistent DST local clocks; all-day schedules retain canonical local-date metadata and their prior anchor time. Missing inherited context returns an explicit valid null no-op, while known occurrence bases are preserved. Pending merge clones typed components, preserves date/clock parts when only the other changes and retains explicit relative-offset/inherit semantics without mutating drafts. This resolver interprets structured components rather than conversational text.

A scoped own-entry weekday lookup now rejects constructor/__proto__ as INVALID_WEEKDAY; the preceding implementation let those inherited values reach arithmetic and reported INVALID_CALENDAR_DATE. Valid weekday and clock policy is unchanged. Existing timezone primitive/invalid-context behavior remains injected, and no effect/ownership/current-state certificate is inferred from a resolved date. The offline evaluator adds actual typed temporal source and its entry as read-only orchestration provenance without evaluating host temporal/effect services.

## Phase 5 increment: typed day bounds and locale/timezone inference

The remaining implementations in time.js now live in strict TypeScript. createDayQueryBounds owns getDayBounds through an explicit calendar resolver port, preserving full-day/date-prefix delegation, native target coercion and the legacy next_event null-end exception. locale-timezone-inference.ts owns the unchanged phone/BSUID, NANP, city-text, nearest-city and GPS-box algorithms and catalogues. Runtime behavior is preserved. Spanish BSUID membership and phone prefixes determine the initial locale independently of timezone defaults; they do not lock conversation language. NANP matching retains Boise/Pacific/Mountain/Central/Eastern priority and unknown-code Eastern fallback. City-text matching retains accent removal and first-map-entry precedence, including multiple-location text. Coordinates retain Number coercion, approximate nearest-city lookup with rounded distance up to 250 km before bounding-box fallback, and fresh returned projections. Catalogues, bounds and sets are unchanged. These heuristics provide defaults, not authoritative location evidence. Unsupported day-scope semantics and permissive calendar delegation remain compatible rather than becoming a new admission rule. Comments no longer claim an unmeasured reverse-geocoding latency.

## Phase 5 increment: typed short-lived working-memory cache

createWorkingMemoryStore now owns the complete working-memory store implementation in strict TypeScript: identity lookup aliases, item normalization, short-lived state, notification merging, snapshots/history restoration and bounded reference prompt. Prompts show at most ten positioned references and preserve fault/container warnings without rendering internal IDs directly. This cache never establishes current inventory, ownership or durable effect evidence.

Checkout builders, canonical-plan resolution, live provider configuration and warning logging are explicit host ports. reminder-pro.js retains the actual Stripe/Lemon/Mercado Pago integrations and stable exports; its declaration derives the typed selector/factory surface. The implementation is a runtime compiler root. Typed and host sources are evaluator provenance only, with two source-mutation cases proving hash changes without executing checkout/plan ports.

Provider selection preserves configured case-insensitive overrides and the existing Colombian phone/BSUID/default routing. It remains pure and reads no environment or checkout service. Context resolution reads configuration at each call and invokes only selected provider ports. Stripe retains full/short URL and short-first fallback; Lemon retains explicit country or existing phone/timezone/language country inference. Mercado Pago retains the legacy fallback, one canonical-plan attempt, original init_point/checkout_url/ID observations, truthy finite amount parsing and currency coercion. Numeric zero is skipped by the existing truthiness check, while string zero is parsed. Plan failures log and return existing fallback/observed context without replay; local Stripe/Lemon builder errors propagate without switching provider. Conditional output fields and existing metadata remain unchanged.

These are shared commercial context projections, not checkout authorization, successful payment/subscription/access claims or independent transactional proof. Actual tool capability gates and provider SDK/persistence behavior remain upstream. Existing hardcoded amounts/currencies are preserved, without asserting current vendor pricing. The opaque plan assertion describes consumed host metadata and adds no validation. No live plan, checkout or payment is created by validation.

## Phase 5 increment: typed complete Google auth lifecycle

createGoogleAuth migrates the entire Google authentication module into strict TypeScript: callback selection, credential eligibility/error classification, invalid-integration cleanup, OAuth client/consent/branded URL construction, code exchange and optional user-info projection, authenticated client hydration, refresh-event persistence and remote/local revocation. Live environment/clock, SDK client creation/user-info and integration read/save/delete are explicit host ports. auth.js retains only the actual googleapis/database adapters and stable exports. auth.d.ts derives the public factory methods using the real SDK OAuth2Client type, preserving downstream Google API compatibility. Token types represent nullable access/refresh/expiry and SDK token metadata, and exchange returns the original token object. The module is a runtime compiler root; evaluator hashes the typed source read-only while its existing synthetic Google prompt adapter still prevents SDK execution.

Callback policy preserves the application default, accepted HTTP/HTTPS callback suffix with no query/hash, trailing-slash handling, live environment and one warning per instance for invalid configured redirects. Actual host singleton lifetime remains unchanged. Access-only integrations are unusable at the sixty-second expiry boundary; refresh credentials retain recovery, and invalid/missing expiry keeps existing behavior. Error classification preserves explicit 401/GOOGLE_AUTH_REQUIRED and known credential/scopes payloads without treating a generic quota/resource 403 as authentication failure. Existing payload coercion and cyclic-serialization fallback remain intact. Consent scopes and state user/phone/time encoding are unchanged; this migration adds no callback/state authorization mechanism.

Code exchange sets the returned tokens before the optional profile read, retaining tokens when user-info fails and propagating exchange failures without replay. Authenticated clients clear only expired access-only integrations and preserve explicit undefined expiry on hydration. Token listeners save fresh fields with original access/refresh fallbacks, expiry conversion, scopes, account email and metadata; save failures log without retry. Nullable/missing access data still reaches the original persistence guard rather than acquiring a new success assertion. Remote revocation prefers the refresh token and runs before local deletion. Remote failure logs but does not skip deletion; delete errors propagate for revoke, while invalid-integration cleanup returns false on ordinary cleanup failures. No SDK/storage capability or independently certified effect evidence is added.

Fourteen typed cases plus one provenance case cover live redirects/warning lifetime, expiry/refresh boundaries, specific versus generic errors, configured clients, exact consent state/scopes, branded queries, exchange/profile ordering/errors, expired cleanup, refresh-only hydration, token persistence/failures, revoke/delete ordering and existing empty-user/absent-integration results. 672 complete ten-API comparisons against the preceding JS preserve outputs, SDK/storage/log calls, token/state/metadata values, refreshed fallbacks and error behavior across four environments, eight integration states, seven SDK/storage modes and three phone inputs, with fixed time and synthetic ports.

Production Docker build and network-disabled Node 22.23.3 smoke pass without compiler/tsx/development workspaces. Actual auth facade and googleapis construct a real SDK client and consent URL locally under synthetic configuration; the SDK's additional getAccessToken method and exact state fields remain available. Injected lifecycle ports verify fresh-token persistence/original metadata, expiry conversion, remote-revoke-before-local-delete despite remote failure and access-only expiry. No live code exchange, token refresh request, revocation or storage effect occurs. Accumulated router/gateway checks retain 128 assembly comparisons, three synthetic fetches and zero storage/RPC/live HTTP calls. The previous 32MiB /tmp tmpfs workaround remains, without image/data cleanup. These are offline boundary checks, not full server boot or a live Google authorization cycle. No SQL boundary changed, so no new PostgreSQL fixture is required.

## Phase 5 increment: typed subscription policy and persistence

createSubscriptionPolicy and createSubscriptionPersistence migrate the full subscription module into strict TypeScript. Optional payment/cancellation fields retain their original truthy-only update behavior. Already-cancelled/missing-user/missing-subscription results remain unchanged. Returned rows remain observations, not independent proof of remote cancellation or cross-service atomicity.

## Phase 5 increment: typed checkout clients and shared database initialization

createStripeClient and createLemonSqueezyClient move the remaining local URL/HMAC logic into strict TypeScript, with live environment/logging ports and an injected Stripe clock.
